user
Assess this manuscript's publication potential honestly. You are not its author and you gain nothing from flattering it.

Read main.tex and its section files, the artifacts/ verification scripts and their output, and any oracle_sprint_*.md replies in artifacts/ (those are an external expert's rounds, already filtered by a verifier).

Answer these, in order, in ENGLISH, concisely:

1. HEADLINE RESULT. State the single strongest theorem in one sentence, as a mathematician would state it. Give its label.

2. IS IT ACTUALLY NEW? Judge independently. Search arXiv/MathSciNet-style knowledge for the nearest prior work and name it. Then classify the headline as one of:
   - GENUINELY NEW (no prior work does this)
   - INCREMENTAL (a known result extended by a step)
   - REPACKAGING (known content in new notation)
   Justify in two sentences with the specific prior work.

3. DEPTH OF THE MATHEMATICS. Is the proof technique substantial (new mechanism, hard estimate, genuine obstruction overcome), or is it bookkeeping/computation? Be blunt.

4. TOP-JOURNAL VERDICT. Could this, as it stands, plausibly be accepted at a top-tier journal in its field (Annals/Inventiones/JAMS tier = TIER-1; JFA/ETDS/Trans.AMS/Math.Comp tier = TIER-2; solid specialist journal = TIER-3; borderline/niche = TIER-4)? Give ONE tier and one sentence of justification. Do not inflate.

5. WHAT WOULD MOVE IT UP ONE TIER. The single most valuable missing theorem or strengthening, stated precisely. If nothing realistic would move it up, say so.

6. WEAKEST LINK. The claim most likely to draw a referee's objection, and why.

Be concise: at most 30 lines total. No praise, no hedging filler.

## Instruction Priority

Superpowers skills override default system prompt behavior, but **user instructions always take precedence**:

1. **User's explicit instructions** (CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, AGENTS.md, direct requests) â€” highest priority
2. **Superpowers skills** â€” override default system behavior where they conflict
3. **Default system prompt** â€” lowest priority

If CLAUDE.md, GEMINI.md, or AGENTS.md says "don't use TDD" and a skill says "always use TDD," follow the user's instructions. The user is in control.

## How to Access Skills

**In Claude Code:** Use the `Skill` tool. When you invoke a skill, its content is loaded and presented to youâ€”follow it directly. Never use the Read tool on skill files.

**In Copilot CLI:** Use the `skill` tool. Skills are auto-discovered from installed plugins. The `skill` tool works the same as Claude Code's `Skill` tool.

**In Gemini CLI:** Skills activate via the `activate_skill` tool. Gemini loads skill metadata at session start and activates the full content on demand.

**In other environments:** Check your platform's documentation for how skills are loaded.

## Platform Adaptation

Skills use Claude Code tool names. Non-CC platforms: see `references/copilot-tools.md` (Copilot CLI), `references/codex-tools.md` (Codex) for tool equivalents. Gemini CLI users get the tool mapping loaded automatically via GEMINI.md.

# Using Skills

## The Rule

**Invoke relevant or requested skills BEFORE any response or action.** Even a 1% chance a skill might apply means that you should invoke the skill to check. If an invoked skill turns out to be wrong for the situation, you don't need to use it.

```dot
digraph skill_flow {
    "User message received" [shape=doublecircle];
    "About to EnterPlanMode?" [shape=doublecircle];
    "Already brainstormed?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" [shape=box];
    "Might any skill apply?" [shape=diamond];
    "Invoke Skill tool" [shape=box];
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" [shape=box];
    "Has checklist?" [shape=diamond];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [shape=box];
    "Follow skill exactly" [shape=box];
    "Respond (including clarifications)" [shape=doublecircle];

    "About to EnterPlanMode?" -> "Already brainstormed?";
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Invoke brainstorming skill" [label="no"];
    "Already brainstormed?" -> "Might any skill apply?" [label="yes"];
    "Invoke brainstorming skill" -> "Might any skill apply?";

    "User message received" -> "Might any skill apply?";
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Invoke Skill tool" [label="yes, even 1%"];
    "Might any skill apply?" -> "Respond (including clarifications)" [label="definitely not"];
    "Invoke Skill tool" -> "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'";
    "Announce: 'Using [skill] to [purpose]'" -> "Has checklist?";
    "Has checklist?" -> "Create TodoWrite todo per item" [label="yes"];
    "Has checklist?" -> "Follow skill exactly" [label="no"];
    "Create TodoWrite todo per item" -> "Follow skill exactly";
}
```

## Red Flags

These thoughts mean STOPâ€”you're rationalizing:

| Thought | Reality |
|---------|---------|
| "This is just a simple question" | Questions are tasks. Check for skills. |
| "I need more context first" | Skill check comes BEFORE clarifying questions. |
| "Let me explore the codebase first" | Skills tell you HOW to explore. Check first. |
| "I can check git/files quickly" | Files lack conversation context. Check for skills. |
| "Let me gather information first" | Skills tell you HOW to gather information. |
| "This doesn't need a formal skill" | If a skill exists, use it. |
| "I remember this skill" | Skills evolve. Read current version. |
| "This doesn't count as a task" | Action = task. Check for skills. |
| "The skill is overkill" | Simple things become complex. Use it. |
| "I'll just do this one thing first" | Check BEFORE doing anything. |
| "This feels productive" | Undisciplined action wastes time. Skills prevent this. |
| "I know what that means" | Knowing the concept â‰  using the skill. Invoke it. |

## Skill Priority

When multiple skills could apply, use this order:

1. **Process skills first** (brainstorming, debugging) - these determine HOW to approach the task
2. **Implementation skills second** (frontend-design, mcp-builder) - these guide execution

"Let's build X" â†’ brainstorming first, then implementation skills.
"Fix this bug" â†’ debugging first, then domain-specific skills.

## Skill Types

**Rigid** (TDD, debugging): Follow exactly. Don't adapt away discipline.

**Flexible** (patterns): Adapt principles to context.

The skill itself tells you which.

## User Instructions

Instructions say WHAT, not HOW. "Add X" or "Fix Y" doesn't mean skip workflows.

\title[Gluing Obstructions and Homological Visibility]{Gluing Obstructions and Homological Visibility in a State-Forcing Semantics}
\author{Haobo Ma}
\address[Haobo Ma]{AELF PTE LTD., \#14-02, Marina Bay Financial Centre Tower 1, 8 Marina Blvd, Singapore 018981}
\email[Haobo Ma]{auric@aelf.io}

\author{Wenlin Zhang}
\address[Wenlin Zhang]{National University of Singapore, Singapore}
\email[Wenlin Zhang]{e1327962@u.nus.edu}

\begin{document}

\begin{abstract}
We study gluing failure in a forcing semantics whose states are sets of realizations. For admitted references, compatible local sectionability is equivalent, on slice-separated reference slices, to nonemptiness of the sheafification of the associated local-object functor. A lower-fragment separation theorem shows that compatible local sections, global sections, and gluing-level nullity are undefinable in the automorphism-invariant, constant-free, one-variable fragment of the lower reduct. At the groupoid-valued level, representative rigidity and global conservativity force surjectivity of the sheafification unit at the terminal fibre; with $H^1$-vanishing this is equivalent to the local object functor already being a sheaf there. Hence gluing-level nullity cannot be recovered from a strict functorial choice of local representatives. For banded realization-stack extensions, each visible branch determines a canonical Giraud class in $H^2$. For finite abelian bands this yields a homological evaluation map whose image controls a presentation-object visible-quotient invariant, while the character-blind case is exactly the pure $\operatorname{Ext}$ contribution. On fixed finite nerves with $H_1=0$ and $H_2\cong\mathbb Z^\beta$, mixed realizations produce precisely the hidden subgroups generated by at most $\beta$ elements; on bouquet good covers, a nonzero finite abelian group occurs as a pure two-branch resolution kernel exactly when $d(G)\le 2\beta$ and $G$ is not a cyclic $p$-group.
\end{abstract}

\keywords{state forcing, local objects, sheafification, gerbes, visible quotient, universal coefficient theorem}
\subjclass[2020]{03B45, 03F55, 03G30, 18F20}

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}

Sheaf-theoretic semantics offers a natural language for the passage from local data to global objects. In logical applications, and in particular in sheaf-theoretic analyses of contextuality \cite{AbramskyBrandenburger2011,AbramskyMansfieldSoaresBarbosa2012,AbramskyBarbosaKishidaLalMansfield2015,Caru2017,Caru2018,Montanhano2021}, the central question is whether compatible local families glue to global sections. The classical tools for measuring the failure of gluing are gerbes, obstruction classes, and cohomological invariants \cite{Giraud1971,Breen2006,Hatcher2002}. In this paper we study that question in a forcing semantics whose states are sets of realizations, with the aim of separating the successive layers at which genuinely new local-to-global structure appears.

Our starting point is the extension chain
\[
\mathbb{L}_0 \xhookrightarrow{\iota} \mathbb{L}_1 \preceq \mathbb{L}_2 \preceq \mathbb{L}_3 \preceq \mathbb{L}_4,
\]
where $\mathbb{L}_0$ is the pointwise layer, $\mathbb{L}_1$ is the information-state forcing layer, $\mathbb{L}_2$ adds local objects and site structure, $\mathbb{L}_3$ adds branchwise obstruction data, and $\mathbb{L}_4$ adds refinement dynamics. The map $\mathbb{L}_0\hookrightarrow\mathbb{L}_1$ is a singleton semantic embedding, whereas the later steps are conservative extensions in the sense of \Cref{def:conservative-extension}. Thus the geometric and homological material introduced later does not alter the forcing behaviour of formulas already present in the lower layers.
The semantics is thus related both to team and imperfect-information semantics \cite{Hodges1997,Vaananen2007,AbramskyPuljujarviVaananen2025} and to the forcing and sheaf-theoretic tradition stemming from Beth, Kripke, Fitting, and Tierney and later developed in topos-theoretic form \cite{Beth1956,Kripke1965,Fitting1969,Tierney1972,Goldblatt2006,Johnstone2002}.

The first part of the paper concerns the passage from information states to local objects. \Cref{prop:compsec-to-sheafification} shows that compatible local sectionability gives nonemptiness of the associated sheafification, and \Cref{thm:sheafification-characterization} proves the converse on slice-separated reference slices. We then prove in \Cref{thm:pointwise-irreducibility} that the predicates $\CompSec_s$, $\Sec_s$, and $\Null^{\mathrm{glue}}_s$ are not definable by automorphism-invariant, constant-free formulas of the $\mathbb{L}_1$-reduct with one free reference variable. This is only a lower-bound statement for the comparison fragment under consideration, but it isolates a genuine change of behaviour between $\mathbb L_1$ and $\mathbb L_2$.

The second part begins once the set-valued local-object functor is enriched by groupoid-valued data. \Cref{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity} gives a rigidity statement for representative-rigid lifts: if the local-object presheaf is slice-separated and a realization prestack admits a strict functorial choice of representatives and is globally conservative at the terminal fibre, then the sheafification unit is surjective there; under $H^1$-vanishing and the same slice-separatedness, this is equivalent to the local object functor already being a sheaf at the terminal fibre. Thus gluing-level nullity is necessarily tied to genuinely gluing-sensitive lift data. \Cref{thm:intrinsic-branch-obstruction-extension} identifies the canonicity that survives at this level: once the sheafified local object functor has been lifted to a banded realization stack, the branch obstruction map to derived $H^2$ is intrinsic up to equivalence of banded extensions. For such an extension, visible value classes are the global sections of the sheaf of connected components (\Cref{thm:visible-value-components}), each visible branch determines a banded component gerbe (\Cref{thm:component-gerbe-decomposition}), and, on slice-separated reference slices, \Cref{thm:gerbe-null-semantics} identifies gluing-level absence with the existence of visible branches together with non-neutrality of the corresponding component gerbes. No general existence theorem for gluing-sensitive lifts is proved; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-ghz} gives an explicit GHZ/Mermin example of chosen lift data.

The homological part of the paper starts only after one visible branch, one finite nerve presentation, and one constant finite abelian band have been fixed. From that point the tools are classical: the universal coefficient sequence, finite-abelian duality, and standard quotient arguments. What matters for the semantics, at a fixed presentation, is the presentation-object image
\[
K_\omega:=\operatorname{Im}(\mathrm{ev}_\omega)
\]
and quotient $A_{\mathrm{vis}}^\omega=A/K_\omega$ attached to the branch obstruction class. \Cref{prop:invariance-visible-quotients} establishes invariance under elementary homology-equivalent refinements, cocycle replacement, and re-trivialization of the band, and gives functorial transport along chosen finite comparison zigzags; it does not assert path independence between distinct zigzags. \Cref{thm:intrinsic-visible-quotient} gives the corresponding character-theoretic description, and \Cref{thm:character-blind-obstructions} identifies the character-blind case with the pure $\operatorname{Ext}$ contribution.

When several visible branches are present, one obtains branch-sensitive and branch-uniform quotients, related by the short exact sequence of \Cref{thm:branch-comparison-exact-sequence}. On a generated pullback subsite for a fixed finite nerve, with constant finite abelian band, the explicit finite-site Leray comparison of \Cref{lem:generated-subsite-model-interface}, and the additional assumption $H_1(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)\allowbreak=0$, we prove realization theorems for prescribed branchwise homomorphisms $H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)\allowbreak\to A$. These theorems are internal constructions under the generated admissible-expansion convention of \Cref{prop:admissible-layer-expansion-interface}: the algebraic data are built first, and the one-state $\mathbb L_2$- and $\mathbb L_3$-models are then obtained by admissible expansion. \Cref{thm:prescribed-branch-realization} treats non-neutral branches, while \Cref{thm:mixed-branch-realization} allows arbitrary finite families and identifies the neutral branches with the zero maps.

Under the same fixed finite-nerve, constant-band, $H_1=0$, and mixed-realization hypotheses, if $H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)\cong \mathbb Z^\beta$, \Cref{cor:torsion-free-h2-classification} shows that the realizable hidden subgroups are exactly the subgroups of $A$ generated by at most $\beta$ elements. The bouquet theorem \Cref{thm:bouquet-two-branch-resolution} then supplies an explicit chosen family of examples, namely open-star good covers of a fixed finite model of $\bigvee_{j=1}^{\beta}S^2$, for which the acyclic-cover hypothesis is automatic and the pure two-branch resolution kernels can be classified explicitly: a finite abelian group $G$ occurs precisely when $d(G)\le 2\beta$ and $G$ is not a cyclic $p$-group. This is an existence and classification statement inside the chosen good-cover construction, not a uniqueness assertion for all presentations of the bouquet. In the unique-branch case this yields a direct comparison with the usual no-global-section picture from sheaf-theoretic contextuality; see \Cref{thm:unique-branch-contextuality-comparison}.

The sheaf-theoretic, gerbe-theoretic, and homological tools used here are classical, and several of the later comparison results are formal once the branchwise intrinsic data have been fixed. The contribution of the paper is accordingly structural: the extension chain separates the semantic layers, the dependence on extra realization data is made explicit, and the obstruction class gives rise to a groupoid-indexed invariant at fixed finite nerve presentations, with functorial transport along chosen finite comparison zigzags inside the controlled finite-nerve comparison groupoid.

The paper is organized as follows. \Cref{sec:preliminaries} fixes the extension chain and the relevant notions of conservativity. \Cref{sec:information-states} recalls forcing over information states. \Cref{sec:null-decomposition} studies local objects, sheafification, and lower-fragment separation. \Cref{sec:gerbe-obstruction} introduces component gerbes and branch obstruction classes. \Cref{sec:homological-visibility} and \Cref{sec:branch-aggregation} contain the homological visibility theory together with the branchwise comparison and realization results. The appendix collects the supplementary material on refinement dynamics and the GHZ/Mermin example.

\section{Concluding remarks}\label{sec:conclusion}

The paper separates three stages of the local-to-global problem. At the level of information states, \Cref{thm:pointwise-irreducibility} shows that compatible local sectionability and gluing-level nullity are invisible in the automorphism-invariant, constant-free, one-variable fragment considered here. This is not intended as a general expressivity theorem, but it does isolate the first place at which the local-object layer goes beyond the lower forcing language.

At the passage from set-valued to groupoid-valued data, \Cref{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity} shows that a slice-separated representative-rigid lift can be globally conservative only when the sheafification unit at the terminal fibre is surjective; under $H^1$-vanishing and slice-separatedness on the reference slice, this is equivalent to sheafiness of the local object functor at that fibre. Thus the gerbe-theoretic part of the theory is necessarily relative to additional lift data. \Cref{thm:intrinsic-branch-obstruction-extension} identifies the maximal canonicity available without an existence theorem: once the sheafified local object functor has been lifted to a banded realization stack, the branch obstruction class is intrinsic up to equivalence of such extensions. Visible value classes are then governed by the sheaf of connected components; on slice-separated reference slices, strict gluing failure is carried by the component gerbes of the visible branches.

For a finite abelian branch obstruction class, the presentation-object
subgroup \(K_\omega=\operatorname{Im}(\mathrm{ev}_\omega)\) and quotient
\(A_{\mathrm{vis}}^\omega=A/K_\omega\) capture the homological information
that survives at a fixed finite nerve presentation; across presentations
they form a groupoid-indexed invariant transported along chosen morphisms in
the finite nerve comparison groupoid. The branchwise comparison sequence and
the realization theorems show how these quotients behave when several
visible branches are present, under the fixed finite nerve, constant-band,
explicit \v{C}ech--derived comparison package, and \(H_1=0\) hypotheses used
in the construction. When in addition
\(H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)\cong\mathbb Z^\beta\), the realizable hidden
subgroups are exactly the subgroups generated by at most \(\beta\) elements.
The bouquet construction supplies an explicit chosen class of good-cover
examples in which the pure two-branch resolution kernels can be described
completely: the nonzero finite abelian groups that occur are exactly those
with \(d(G)\le2\beta\) which are not cyclic \(p\)-groups. The appendix
contains only supplementary material: the refinement-dynamic extension,
finite-state complexity bounds, and the GHZ/Mermin lift used as an
illustrative chosen datum.

The hypothesis package is therefore deliberately visible: \textup{(RC)} for
local objects, \textup{(H1)}--\textup{(H4)} and global conservativity for
component gerbes, slice-separatedness whenever visible branches are used as
strict compatible local sections, slice-separatedness and $H^1$-vanishing for
terminal-fibre rigidity, and finite-nerve plus constant-band data together
with the explicit \v{C}ech--derived comparison package for homological
visibility.
This paper keeps that package in the pullback-based slice-site formulation; the
meet-semilattice presentation belongs to the companion APAL article. The
comparison between presentations is consequently groupoid-indexed and
zigzag-dependent, not a claim of presentation-free canonical representatives.

# Theorem Inventory

This Stage A inventory was prepared against `scope_contract.md`, `scope_contract.json`, `research_directive.md`, the compiled manuscript sources included by `main.tex`, all `.tex` files in the paper directory, and `references.bib`.

## Summary

The compiled manuscript contains the required A/B/C theorem chain: extension-chain forcing, pullback-based local sites and sheafification, terminal-fibre rigidity, lower-fragment separation, component gerbes and gerbe-null semantics, strict and intrinsic visible quotients, pure `Ext` character blindness, branch-sensitive versus branch-uniform comparison, finite-nerve realization, the torsion-free `H_2 = Z^beta` subgroup classification, bouquet two-branch resolution, Abramsky-Brandenburger comparison, and the conditional GHZ/Mermin lift.

No required in-scope theorem from the scope contract is completely absent from the compiled manuscript. The main risks are not missing labels but proof-interface sharpness and source hygiene:

- `lem:bouquet-cover-classical-interface` uses `beta` without quantifying it.
- `lem:generated-subsite-model-interface` needs an explicit admissible-model/free-expansion convention.
- `prop:invariance-visible-quotients` must remain groupoid-indexed, not path-independent.
- `sec_forcing_necessity.tex` is an unused stronger draft with duplicate `thm:pointwise-irreducibility` and unresolved references.
- `sequel/` files are out-of-scope split material.
- Several included section files exceed the 800-line discipline.

## Files Written

- `theorem_inventory.json`
- `theorem_inventory.md`

## JSON Inventory

The authoritative machine-readable inventory is in `theorem_inventory.json`. It uses exactly the required top-level keys:

- `valid`
- `in_scope_present`

- `out_of_scope_strong_results`

- `irrelevant_or_remove`
- `naive_truncation_risks`
- `journal_style_gaps`

## Classification Notes

The inventory treats the compiled `main.tex` inputs as the current manuscript. It also scans unused `.tex` files because the Stage A prompt requested all TeX sources. Results in `sequel/` and `sec_forcing_necessity.tex` are therefore classified, but not as part of the compiled article.

Classical material is classified as supporting/background when it is a standard sheaf, stack, gerbe, Cech, UCT, finite abelian duality, or finite group structure fact. The paper-specific contribution is the way these tools are assembled into the state-forcing gluing-obstruction and homological-visibility chain.

The inventory records cross-resolution risks only where a future edit might accidentally convert a stated comparison or monotonicity result into a naive invariant. The current manuscript usually handles this correctly by requiring homology-equivalent refinement, chosen zigzags, Leray/good-cover comparison, or explicit pullback hypotheses.

Target journal: APAL (Annals of Pure and Applied Logic)
Previously: NDJFL (changed after internal review)

Revision summary (2026-03-23):

1. Fixed correctness issue in Theorem 4.26 (component gerbe decomposition):
   The original version claimed the full stack decomposition unconditionally.
   The proof only showed each visible branch gives a gerbe, not that every
   local branch comes from a global one. Fix: the gerbe property is now the
   theorem; the full decomposition is a corollary under an explicit branch
   constancy hypothesis.

2. Fixed correctness issue in Theorem 4.28 (gerbe-null-semantics):
   The equivalence Sec_s(r) <=> L_r(a) nonempty used the direction from
   L_r(a) to Sec_s(r) without justification. Fix: added Definition
   (global conservativity at the terminal fibre) requiring essential
   surjectivity of the stackification morphism on the terminal fibre,
   and Proposition (no new global objects at a) deriving the equivalence.

3. Added forcing necessity theorem (pointwise irreducibility of gluing
   predicates): Two admitted references are constructed that are
   indistinguishable at L_1 but one has CompSec /\ Sec while the other
   has CompSec /\ not Sec. This proves that the local-object enrichment
   is not merely a convenience but introduces genuinely new predicates.

4. Added connection to sheaf-theoretic contextuality: In the unique-branch
   case, the branched gerbe semantics reduces to the standard no-global-
   section picture of Abramsky--Brandenburger. The Ext-residual provides
   a precise mechanism for the incomplete cohomological detection
   identified by Caru.

5. Removed all references to "larger architecture", "broader research
   program", "eleven-layer architecture". The paper is now presented as
   a self-contained four-layer fragment.

6. Moved finite-state complexity upper bounds from main text to appendix.

7. Split sec_null_decomposition.tex (1689 lines) into three files under
   800 lines each.

8. Updated abstract, introduction, preliminaries, and conclusion to
   reflect all changes.

Residual limitations:
1. The paper gives upper bounds for the support problems but not matching
   lower bounds. This is now stated as an open problem.
2. The realization layer remains abstract.

  330: with the following properties:
  331: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  332: \item $\mathfrak P_r\to\mathcal C_{p,s}/a$ is a realization prestack with band $\Band$ in the sense of \Cref{def:realization-prestack};
  333: \item $\mathfrak L_r$ is a $\Band$-banded stack whose connected components agree with the sheafification of $F_{p,s}$, i.e.
  334: \[
  335: \pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)\cong a_{p,s}F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_{p,s}/a};
  336: \]
  337: \item the stackification morphism $\iota_r:\mathfrak P_r\to\mathfrak L_r$ is globally conservative at $a$ in the sense of \Cref{def:global-conservativity};
  338: \item for each visible branch supported by the datum, the induced family
  339: \(\Cov_v^{\mathrm{acyc}}(a)\) is part of a \v{C}ech--derived comparison
  340: package in the sense of \Cref{def:cech-derived-comparison-package}; in
  341: particular, its covers admit \v{C}ech nerves in the slice, trivialize the
  342: band where the obstruction cocycles are computed, and carry the specified
  343: comparison isomorphism to derived cohomology.
  344: \end{enumerate}
  345: If such a datum exists we say that $r$ admits a gluing-sensitive realization. This datum is extra structure on top of the bare local object functor: later sections treat the gerbe-theoretic layer only relative to a chosen datum of this kind and do not assert that every local object functor admits one.
  346: \end{definition}
  347: 
  348: The following result applies the comparison class of \Cref{def:lower-comparison-class} to the local-object predicates.
  349: 
  350: \begin{theorem}[Parameter-free one-reference lower-fragment separation in the constant-free reduct]\label{thm:pointwise-irreducibility}
  351: There exist a model $M$, the singleton information state
  352: \[
  353: p=(\varnothing,\{\emptyset\}),
  354: \]
  355: and pairwise distinct elements
  356: \[
  357: a_1,a_2,a_3,a_4\in D_{\mathsf{Ref}_s}^M
  358: \]
  359: of one reference sort such that:
  360: The comparison language in this theorem is restricted to the
  361: parameter-free one-variable \(\Sigma_1\)-reduct: it excludes admission,
  362: all local-object predicates, site predicates, cover data, and parameters of
  363: reference sort. The undefinability conclusions are therefore conclusions on
  364: the admitted subdomain displayed in \textup{(i)}, not definitions of
  365: admission itself in the lower language.
  366: No separation claim is made for languages with such parameters or with
  367: access to the \(\Sigma_2\) local-object structure.
  368: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  369: \item each $a_i$ is admitted at $p$, and for every $\varphi(x)\in\mathcal{C}_{s}^{\mathrm{inv}}$,
  370: \[
  371: M,\emptyset[x\mapsto a_i]\models\varphi(x)
  372: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  373: M,\emptyset[x\mapsto a_j]\models\varphi(x)
  374: \]
  375: for all $i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\}$;
  376: \item
  377: \[
  378: M,p\Vdash \bigl(\CompSec_s(x)\land \Sec_s(x)\bigr)[a_1/x];
  379: \]
  380: \item
  381: \[
  382: M,p\Vdash \bigl(\CompSec_s(x)\land \neg\Sec_s(x)\bigr)[a_2/x];
  383: \]
  384: \item
  385: \[
  386: M,p\Vdash \bigl(\LocSec_s(x)\land \neg\CompSec_s(x)\bigr)[a_3/x];
  387: \]
  388: \item
  389: \[
  390: M,p\Vdash \bigl(\neg\LocSec_s(x)\bigr)[a_4/x].
  391: \]
  392: \end{enumerate}
  393: Consequently, on the displayed admitted subdomain, no $\varphi(x)\in\mathcal{C}_{s}^{\mathrm{inv}}$ can define any of the predicates
  394: \[
  395: \LocSec_s,
  396: \qquad
  397: \CompSec_s,
  398: \qquad
  399: \Sec_s,
  400: \qquad
  401: \Null^{\mathrm{loc}}_s,
  402: \qquad
  403: \Null^{\mathrm{cmp}}_s,
  404: \qquad
  405: \Null^{\mathrm{glue}}_s
  406: \]
  407: on admitted reference elements. In particular, $\CompSec_s$, $\Sec_s$, and $\Null^{\mathrm{glue}}_s$ are not reducible to the automorphism-invariant pointwise fragment of the $\mathbb L_1$-reduct.
  408: \end{theorem}
  409: 
  410: \begin{proof}
  411: Take one referenceable sort $s$ and let the lower-layer state be the singleton
  412: \[
  413: p=(\varnothing,\{\emptyset\}).
  414: \]
  415: Choose the constant-free $\Sigma_1$-reduct of $M$ so that the reference carrier of sort $\mathsf{Ref}_s$ is
  416: \[
  417: D_{\mathsf{Ref}_s}^M=\{(i,n)\mid i\in\{1,2,3,4\},\ n\in\mathbb N\},
  418: \]
  419: and write $a_i:=(i,0)$. Interpret the remaining symbols of the constant-free $\Sigma_1$-reduct so that no interpretation depends on the first coordinate on this sort: relations involving $\mathsf{Ref}_s$ are chosen to be unions of whole first-coordinate orbits, and functions with arguments in $\mathsf{Ref}_s$ are chosen to factor through the second coordinate or to be constant on that sort. Then every permutation of $\{1,2,3,4\}$ on the first coordinate, fixing the second coordinate, extends to an automorphism of the reduct. In particular, for any $i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\}$ there exists an automorphism $\sigma$ sending $a_i$ to $a_j$. Since formulas in $\mathcal{C}_{s}^{\mathrm{inv}}$ are parameter free and automorphism invariant, \textup{(i)} follows immediately.
  420: 
  421: Now attach the $\Sigma_2$-data. Declare each $a_i$ admitted, so
  422: \[
  423: M,p\Vdash \Adm_s(x)[a_i/x]
  424: \qquad
  425: (1\le i\le 4).
  426: \]
  427: Let the local reference site $\mathcal C_{p,s}$ be the disjoint union of four rooted thin components, one over each admitted class represented by $a_i$. In the first three components, write the root as $a_i$ and choose objects
  428: \[
  429: c_i\sqsubseteq u_i\sqsubseteq a_i,
  430: \qquad
  431: c_i\sqsubseteq v_i\sqsubseteq a_i,
  432: \]
  433: with covering family $\{u_i\to a_i,v_i\to a_i\}$ and pullback $c_i=u_i\times_{a_i}v_i$. In the fourth component, let $a_4$ be the unique object and let the trivial cover $\{a_4\to a_4\}$ be the only covering family.
  434: 
  435: This expansion is deliberately outside the lower comparison language. The $\Sigma_1$-reduct remembers the reference carrier, equality, the lower many-sorted structure, and the singleton information state; it forgets admission, the site, covers, local object functor, and the predicates $\LocSec_s,\CompSec_s,\Sec_s$. Hence the automorphisms of the displayed $\Sigma_1$-reduct are the comparison automorphisms used in \Cref{def:lower-comparison-class}. Since that class contains only constant-free one-variable $\Sigma_1$-formulas and is closed under these reduct automorphisms, any later distinction produced by the $\Sigma_2$-data cannot be read by a formula in $\mathcal C_s^{\mathrm{inv}}$.
  436: 
  437: Define the local object functor $F_{p,s}$ componentwise as follows.
  438: 
  439: For $a_1$, set
  440: \[
  441: F_{p,s}(u_1)=F_{p,s}(v_1)=F_{p,s}(c_1)=F_{p,s}(a_1)=\{*\},
  442: \]
  443: with all restriction maps equal to the identity. Then the unique local family on the chosen cover is compatible and glues to the global section in $F_{p,s}(a_1)$, proving \textup{(ii)}.
  444: 
  445: For $a_2$, set
  446: \[
  447: F_{p,s}(u_2)=F_{p,s}(v_2)=F_{p,s}(c_2)=\{*\},
  448: \qquad
  449: F_{p,s}(a_2)=\varnothing,
  450: \]
  451: again with identity restrictions on the nonempty fibres. Then the local family $(*,*)$ is compatible on the overlap, but there is no global section at $a_2$, proving \textup{(iii)}.
  452: 
  453: For $a_3$, set
  454: \[
  455: F_{p,s}(u_3)=\{\sigma_u\},
  456: \quad
  457: F_{p,s}(v_3)=\{\sigma_v\},
  458: \quad
  459: F_{p,s}(c_3)=\{0,1\},
  460: \quad
  461: F_{p,s}(a_3)=\varnothing,
  462: \]
  463: with
  464: \[
  465: \res_{c_3}^{u_3}(\sigma_u)=0,
  466: \qquad
  467: \res_{c_3}^{v_3}(\sigma_v)=1.
  468: \]
  469: The pretopology on this component is the smallest one generated by the
  470: identity covers, the cover
  471: \[
  472: \{u_3\to a_3,\ v_3\to a_3\},
  473: \]
  474: its pullbacks along arrows in the component, and transitivity. Pulling this
  475: cover back to \(u_3\) gives the cover \(\{u_3\to u_3,\ c_3\to u_3\}\);
  476: pulling it back to \(v_3\) gives \(\{c_3\to v_3,\ v_3\to v_3\}\); pulling it
  477: back to \(c_3\) gives only identity copies of \(c_3\).
  478: 
  479: We claim that every generated cover of \(a_3\) that contains local sections
  480: has, after deleting repeated identity refinements, nonempty refinements over
  481: both \(u_3\) and \(v_3\). This is proved by induction on the generation of
  482: covers. The displayed generator has this property. Pullback stability only
  483: creates covers of \(u_3\), \(v_3\), or \(c_3\), and therefore cannot produce a
  484: new cover of \(a_3\) missing one side. For transitivity, a cover of \(a_3\)
  485: is obtained by refining the \(u_3\)-member and the \(v_3\)-member separately;
  486: if the resulting family has local sections, the refinements over \(u_3\) and
  487: over \(v_3\) must each contain a member on which the unique sections
  488: \(\sigma_u\) and \(\sigma_v\) restrict. These restrictions meet over a
  489: refinement of \(c_3=u_3\times_{a_3}v_3\). On every such refinement the
  490: \(u_3\)-side restriction is \(0\) and the \(v_3\)-side restriction is \(1\).
  491: Thus no generated cover of \(a_3\) with local sections admits a compatible
  492: matching family. Local sections exist, for example on the displayed cover,
  493: but every possible generated cover carrying both sides forces the same
  494: incompatible restrictions on \(c_3\). Hence
  495: \[
  496: M,p\Vdash \LocSec_s(a_3)
  497: \qquad\text{and}\qquad
  498: M,p\nVdash \CompSec_s(a_3),
  499: \]
  500: proving \textup{(iv)}.
  501: 
  502: For $a_4$, set
  503: \[
  504: F_{p,s}(a_4)=\varnothing.
  505: \]
  506: Since the trivial cover is the only cover of $a_4$, there are no local sections anywhere above $a_4$, proving \textup{(v)}.
  507: 
  508: Because all of this geometric data lives in $\Sigma_2\setminus\Sigma_1$, it does not alter the automorphism orbit computed in \textup{(i)}. The four admitted reference elements therefore remain indistinguishable in the lower comparison class while separating the higher predicates: $\LocSec_s$ holds at $a_1,a_2,a_3$ and fails at $a_4$; $\CompSec_s$ holds at $a_1,a_2$ and fails at $a_3,a_4$; and $\Sec_s$ holds only at $a_1$.
  509: To spell out the reduct argument, suppose for instance that $\theta(x)\in\mathcal C_s^{\mathrm{inv}}$ defined $\CompSec_s$ on admitted elements in this expansion. The reduct automorphism sending $a_1$ to $a_3$ would force
  510: \[
  511: M,\emptyset[x\mapsto a_1]\models\theta(x)
  512: \Longleftrightarrow
  513: M,\emptyset[x\mapsto a_3]\models\theta(x),
  514: \]
  515: while the $\Sigma_2$-interpretation gives $\CompSec_s(a_1)$ and $\neg\CompSec_s(a_3)$, a contradiction. The pairs $(a_1,a_2)$ and $(a_2,a_4)$ give the same contradiction for $\Sec_s$ and $\LocSec_s$, respectively. Thus none of these three predicates is definable by a formula from the comparison class.
  516: The three null-mode predicates are Boolean combinations of these same existence predicates:
  517: \begin{align*}
  518: \Null_s^{\mathrm{loc}}&=\Adm_s\land \neg\LocSec_s,\\
  519: \Null_s^{\mathrm{cmp}}&=\Adm_s\land \LocSec_s\land \neg\CompSec_s,\\
  520: \Null_s^{\mathrm{glue}}&=\Adm_s\land \CompSec_s\land \neg\Sec_s.
  521: \end{align*}
  522: Since admission is fixed on all four elements and the lower formulas cannot distinguish the relevant existence predicates, none of the three null-mode predicates is definable either.
  523: \end{proof}
  524: 
  525: \begin{remark}[Why tuples and parameters are excluded]\label{rem:tuple-parameter-limitation}
  526: The restriction to one free variable and no parameters is essential for the argument. If two free reference variables are allowed, equality formulas such as $x_1=x_2$ already distinguish tuples by their equality pattern, so a single orbit no longer witnesses the undefinability of $\CompSec_s$ or $\Sec_s$. Likewise, if parameters of sort $\mathsf{Ref}_s$ are available, a formula of the form $x=b$ isolates a chosen witness unless one first arranges that all witnesses lie outside the parameter support. Thus the comparison class $\mathcal{C}_{s}^{\mathrm{inv}}$ records exactly the fragment in which automorphism-invariant pointwise semantics cannot detect compatible local sectionability.
  527: \end{remark}

 succeeded in 204ms:
    1: The preceding corollary classifies the branchwise hidden subgroups that can occur on a fixed finite nerve with $H_2\cong \mathbb Z^\beta$. The next theorem replaces that abstract nerve by an explicitly chosen bouquet good-cover family and classifies, inside that family and with the stated realization data, the finite abelian groups that occur as pure two-branch resolution kernels.
    2: 
    3: All realization data in this subsection are chosen data: a finite simplicial
    4: model $K_\beta$, the open-star good-cover system, a trivialization of the
    5: constant finite abelian band, and the branch cocycles or homomorphisms used in
    6: \Cref{thm:mixed-branch-realization}. The theorem below is not a canonical
    7: classification over all presentations of the bouquet.
    8: 
    9: \begin{lemma}[Bouquet good-cover and finite-abelian interfaces]\label{lem:bouquet-cover-classical-interface}
   10: Let $K$ be a finite simplicial complex and let $\mathcal U^{(m)}$ be the open-star cover of $\operatorname{Sd}^m K$ on $|K|$. Then:
   11: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
   12: \item every nonempty finite intersection of members of $\mathcal U^{(m)}$ is contractible, and $N(\mathcal U^{(m)})$ is canonically isomorphic to $\operatorname{Sd}^mK$;
   13: \item the covers $\mathcal U^{(m+1)}$ refine $\mathcal U^{(m)}$, and in the pullback site generated by all such open stars, their nonempty intersections, and a strict initial object for empty intersections, the family $(\mathcal U^{(m)})_{m\ge0}$ is filtered and cofinal;
   14: \item for every constant finite abelian group $G$, the Leray--\v{C}ech comparison identifies
   15: \[
   16: \varinjlim_m\check H^2(\mathcal U^{(m)},G)
   17: \cong
   18: H^2(|K|,G),
   19: \]
   20: and the comparison is compatible with the simplicial cohomology of the nerves;
   21: \item for every integer $\beta\ge 1$ and every nonzero finite abelian group $G$, the
   22: group $G$ admits a decomposition
   23: \[
   24: G=G_1\oplus G_2
   25: \]
   26: with $G_1,G_2$ nonzero and $d(G_i)\le\beta$ for $i=1,2$ if and only if $d(G)\le 2\beta$ and $G$ is not a cyclic $p$-group.
   27: \end{enumerate}
   28: \end{lemma}
   29: 
   30: \begin{proof}
   31: For \textup{(i)}, the open star of a vertex of a barycentric subdivision is contractible, and a nonempty finite intersection of such open stars is the open star of the simplex spanned by the corresponding vertices. This is contractible. The nerve of the open-star cover of a barycentric subdivision is the subdivision itself; this is the standard nerve computation for open stars in a simplicial complex \cite[Secs.~2.1, 4G]{Hatcher2002}.
   32: 
   33: For \textup{(ii)}, barycentric subdivision gives a simplicial refinement of the previous subdivision, and each open star in $\operatorname{Sd}^{m+1}K$ lies in an open star of $\operatorname{Sd}^{m}K$. The generated site used here includes a strict initial object $\emptyset$ and declares the pullback of two disjoint members to be $\emptyset$; nonempty pullbacks are the corresponding finite intersections. Thus every pullback demanded by the local-reference-site axioms is represented in the site, while the \v{C}ech cochains still ignore empty intersections because the constant sheaf has only the zero section on $\emptyset$. The pretopology is generated by these covers, their pullbacks, identity covers, and the empty cover of $\emptyset$, so any covering family in the generated site is refined by a common later barycentric open-star cover. This proves filteredness and cofinality.
   34: 
   35: For \textup{(iii)}, the contractibility in \textup{(i)} makes each $\mathcal U^{(m)}$ a Leray cover for the genuine constant sheaf $G$ on the associated formal \v{C}ech site. Empty pullback objects contribute no cochains. The Leray theorem for \v{C}ech cohomology therefore identifies the \v{C}ech cohomology of these covers with the sheaf cohomology of $|K|$, and the nerve identification in \textup{(i)} identifies the same groups with simplicial cohomology of $\operatorname{Sd}^mK$ \cite[Chap.~III, Sec.~4]{Bredon1997}, \cite[Sec.~4G]{Hatcher2002}. The refinement maps are the usual subdivision maps, hence induce cohomology isomorphisms.
   36: 
   37: For \textup{(iv)}, fix an integer $\beta\ge1$. The standing nonzero hypothesis removes the trivial group from the classification; here a cyclic $p$-group means a cyclic group of order $p^n$ with $n\ge1$. First suppose such a decomposition exists. Then $G$ is a direct sum of two nonzero groups, so it is decomposable. The finite abelian indecomposable groups are exactly the cyclic groups of prime-power order \cite[Chap.~12]{DummitFoote2004}. Also
   38: \[
   39: d(G)\le d(G_1)+d(G_2)\le 2\beta.
   40: \]
   41: Conversely, assume $d(G)\le2\beta$ and $G$ is not a cyclic $p$-group. There are two cases: either the minimal number of generators is $m\ge2$, or $m=1$ and the cyclic group has non-prime-power order. If $d(G)=m\ge2$, write an invariant-factor decomposition
   42: \[
   43: G\cong C_{n_1}\oplus\cdots\oplus C_{n_m},
   44: \qquad
   45: n_1\mid n_2\mid\cdots\mid n_m.
   46: \]
   47: With $t=\min\{\beta,m-1\}$, the two sums
   48: \[
   49: G_1=C_{n_1}\oplus\cdots\oplus C_{n_t},
   50: \qquad
   51: G_2=C_{n_{t+1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus C_{n_m}
   52: \]
   53: are nonzero and satisfy $d(G_1)\le\beta$ and $d(G_2)=m-t\le\beta$. If $d(G)=1$, then $G$ is cyclic. Since it is not a cyclic $p$-group, its order has at least two coprime factors $a,b>1$, and the Chinese remainder theorem gives
   54: \[
   55: G\cong \mathbb Z/a\mathbb Z\oplus\mathbb Z/b\mathbb Z,
   56: \]
   57: with both summands nonzero and singly generated. This proves the equivalence.
   58: \end{proof}
   59: 
   60: \begin{lemma}[Bouquet mixed-realization transfer]\label{lem:bouquet-mixed-realization-transfer}
   61: Fix $\beta\ge 1$ and the bouquet site $\mathcal C_a$ built from
   62: $K_\beta$ and the open-star covers $\mathcal U_\beta^{(m)}$ as in
   63: \Cref{thm:bouquet-two-branch-resolution}. Let $G$ be a finite abelian
   64: group, and let $G_1,G_2\le G$ be nonzero subgroups satisfying
   65: \[
   66: d(G_i)\le \beta\qquad(i=1,2).
   67: \]
   68: Then there exist a state $p$, an admitted reference $r:\mathsf{Ref}_s$
   69: inside a model $M$ with $a=[r]_p$, a local object functor on
   70: $\mathcal C_a$, and a realization prestack with constant band $G$ such
   71: that
   72: \[
   73: \Vis_{p,s}(r)=\{v_1,v_2\},
   74: \qquad
   75: K_{v_i}=G_i\quad(i=1,2).
   76: \]
   77: Both component gerbes are non-neutral. If, moreover,
   78: \[
   79: G_1\cap G_2=0,\qquad G_1+G_2=G,
   80: \]
   81: then
   82: \[
   83: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{br}}(r)\cong B_{\mathrm{res}}(r)\cong G,
   84: \qquad
   85: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{unif}}(r)=0,
   86: \qquad
   87: M,p\Vdash \Null_s^{\mathrm{glue}}(r).
   88: \]
   89: \end{lemma}
   90: 
   91: \begin{proof}
   92: By \Cref{lem:bouquet-cover-classical-interface}(i)--(iii), the cover
   93: $\mathcal U_\beta^{(0)}$ is a finite good cover whose nerve is
   94: $K_\beta$, all finite intersections are contractible, the constant band
   95: $G$ is trivial on the cover, and the generated filtered family satisfies
   96: \textup{(H4)}. Since $K_\beta\simeq\bigvee_{j=1}^{\beta}S^2$, we have
   97: \[
   98: H_1\bigl(N(\mathcal U_\beta^{(0)}),\mathbb Z\bigr)=0,
   99: \qquad
  100: H_2\bigl(N(\mathcal U_\beta^{(0)}),\mathbb Z\bigr)\cong \mathbb Z^\beta.
  101: \]
  102: The inequalities $d(G_i)\le\beta$ therefore allow homomorphisms
  103: \[
  104: \phi_i:H_2\bigl(N(\mathcal U_\beta^{(0)}),\mathbb Z\bigr)\to G
  105: \]
  106: with $\operatorname{Im}(\phi_i)=G_i$.
  107: 
  108: Apply \Cref{thm:mixed-branch-realization} to the fixed finite nerve,
  109: constant band $A:=G$, branch index set $\{1,2\}$, and homomorphisms
  110: $\phi_1,\phi_2$. It constructs the asserted one-state realization data
  111: and gives
  112: \[
  113: K_{v_i}=\operatorname{Im}(\phi_i)=G_i.
  114: \]
  115: The model constructed here is therefore admissible for the same reason as in
  116: \Cref{thm:mixed-branch-realization}: it is obtained by the layer-expansion
  117: interface of \Cref{prop:admissible-layer-expansion-interface}.
  118: Since $G_i\ne0$, the same theorem makes both component gerbes
  119: non-neutral.
  120: 
  121: Under the additional direct-sum hypotheses, the branch-sensitive hidden
  122: subgroup intersection is $0$ and the branch-uniform hidden subgroup sum
  123: is all of $G$. The quotient formulas in
  124: \Cref{thm:branch-sensitive-visible-quotient,thm:branch-uniform-visible-quotient,thm:branch-comparison-exact-sequence}
  125: therefore give
  126: \[
  127: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{br}}(r)\cong B_{\mathrm{res}}(r)\cong G,
  128: \qquad
  129: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{unif}}(r)=0.
  130: \]
  131: Finally, because both branches are non-neutral,
  132: \Cref{thm:mixed-branch-realization}(iv) gives
  133: $M,p\Vdash \Null_s^{\mathrm{glue}}(r)$.
  134: \end{proof}
  135: 
  136: \begin{theorem}[Admissible-expansion two-branch resolution on chosen bouquet good covers]\label{thm:bouquet-two-branch-resolution}
  137: Fix an integer $\beta\ge 1$, let $K_\beta$ be a finite connected simplicial complex whose geometric realization is homotopy equivalent to
  138: \[
  139: \bigvee_{j=1}^{\beta} S^2,
  140: \]
  141: and write
  142: \[
  143: X_\beta:=|K_\beta|.
  144: \]
  145: For each $m\ge 0$, let
  146: \[
  147: \mathcal U_\beta^{(m)}
  148: \]
  149: be the open-star cover of the barycentric subdivision $\operatorname{Sd}^m K_\beta$, viewed as a cover of $X_\beta$. Let $\mathcal C_a$ be the pullback site whose objects are $X_\beta$, a strict initial object $\emptyset$, and all nonempty finite intersections of members of the covers $\mathcal U_\beta^{(m)}$; the pullback of two objects over a common target is their nonempty intersection when it is nonempty and is $\emptyset$ otherwise. Covering families are generated by the families $\mathcal U_\beta^{(m)}$, their pullbacks, identity covers, and the empty cover of $\emptyset$.
  150: All admissible-expansion realization assertions in this theorem are understood relative to the
  151: well-typed admissible-expansion convention of
  152: \Cref{prop:admissible-layer-expansion-interface}.
  153: 
  154: Then the family
  155: \[
  156: \bigl(\mathcal U_\beta^{(m)}\bigr)_{m\ge 0}
  157: \]
  158: is a filtered cofinal family satisfying \textup{(H4)} for every constant finite abelian band.
  159: 
  160: Let $G$ be a finite abelian group, and write $d(G)$ for the minimal number of generators of $G$. Then the following are equivalent.
  161: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  162: \item $G\ne0$, $d(G)\le 2\beta$, and $G$ is not a cyclic $p$-group, where
  163: cyclic $p$-group means cyclic of order $p^n$ with $n\ge1$.
  164: \item In the admissible-expansion model class generated by
  165: \Cref{prop:admissible-layer-expansion-interface}, the one-state
  166: \(\mathbb L_1\)-reduct, the site \(\mathcal C_a\), a local object functor on
  167: \(\mathcal C_a\), and a realization prestack
  168: \[
  169: \mathfrak P_r\to \mathcal C_a
  170: \]
  171: with trivialized constant band $G$ define a one-state
  172: \(\mathbb L_2/\mathbb L_3\) model \(M\) with state \(p\) and admitted
  173: reference \(r:\mathsf{Ref}_s\), \(a=[r]_p\), using the finite-nerve Leray
  174: comparison supplied by the chosen bouquet good covers, such that
  175: \[
  176: \Vis_{p,s}(r)=\{v_1,v_2\},
  177: \]
  178: both component gerbes $\mathfrak G_{v_1}$ and $\mathfrak G_{v_2}$ are non-neutral, and
  179: \[
  180: K_{v_1}\cap K_{v_2}=0,
  181: \qquad
  182: K_{v_1}+K_{v_2}=G.
  183: \]
  184: \end{enumerate}
  185: Whenever these conditions hold,
  186: \[
  187: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{br}}(r)\cong B_{\mathrm{res}}(r)\cong G,
  188: \qquad
  189: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{unif}}(r)=0,
  190: \qquad
  191: M,p\Vdash \Null_s^{\mathrm{glue}}(r).
  192: \]
  193: \end{theorem}
  194: 
  195: \begin{proof}
  196: We use four explicit interfaces: the good-cover comparison, the
  197: finite-abelian decomposition, the mixed-realization transfer, and the
  198: branch-exactness consequence.
  199: 
  200: The good-cover interface is supplied by
  201: \Cref{lem:bouquet-cover-classical-interface}. By clause \textup{(i)},
  202: each $\mathcal U_\beta^{(m)}$ is a finite good cover of $X_\beta$, its
  203: nonempty finite intersections are contractible, and
  204: \[
  205: N(\mathcal U_\beta^{(m)})\cong \operatorname{Sd}^{m}K_\beta.
  206: \]
  207: Thus the constant band $G$ is trivial on every such cover, and
  208: \[
  209: H^1(U_{i_0\cdots i_n},G)=0
  210: \]
  211: for every nonempty intersection appearing in the \v{C}ech descent
  212: argument. Clause \textup{(ii)} makes
  213: $\bigl(\mathcal U_\beta^{(m)}\bigr)_{m\ge 0}$ filtered and cofinal in the
  214: generated pretopology of $\mathcal C_a$, and clause \textup{(iii)}
  215: identifies the filtered \v{C}ech system with derived cohomology. Hence
  216: the family satisfies \textup{(H4)} for every constant finite abelian band.
  217: 
  218: Also, every subdivision of $K_\beta$ is homotopy equivalent to $K_\beta$, hence
  219: \[
  220: H_1\bigl(N(\mathcal U_\beta^{(m)}),\mathbb Z\bigr)=0,
  221: \qquad
  222: H_2\bigl(N(\mathcal U_\beta^{(m)}),\mathbb Z\bigr)\cong \mathbb Z^\beta
  223: \]
  224: for every $m$ \cite[Secs.~2.1, 2.2]{Hatcher2002}. This records exactly
  225: the finite-nerve hypotheses needed by the realization theorems.
  226: 
  227: Now assume \textup{(i)}. The finite-abelian interface is
  228: \Cref{lem:bouquet-cover-classical-interface}(iv): it gives a direct-sum
  229: decomposition
  230: \[
  231: G=G_1\oplus G_2
  232: \]
  233: with $G_1,G_2$ nonzero and
  234: \[
  235: d(G_1)\le\beta,
  236: \qquad
  237: d(G_2)\le\beta.
  238: \]
  239: The mixed-realization transfer
  240: \Cref{lem:bouquet-mixed-realization-transfer} applied to these two
  241: summands produces a realization with two visible branches, non-neutral
  242: component gerbes, and $K_{v_i}=G_i$. Because the summands form an
  243: internal direct sum, $G_1\cap G_2=0$ and $G_1+G_2=G$, so condition
  244: \textup{(ii)} follows.
  245: 
  246: Conversely, assume \textup{(ii)} and put
  247: \[
  248: K_i:=K_{v_i}
  249: \qquad
  250: (i=1,2).
  251: \]
  252: For each $i$, choose a finite nerve presentation of the branch class $\omega_{v_i}$ on one of the covers $\mathcal U_\beta^{(m_i)}$. Since the good-cover interface gives
  253: \[
  254: H_2\bigl(N(\mathcal U_\beta^{(m_i)}),\mathbb Z\bigr)\cong \mathbb Z^\beta,
  255: \]
  256: the hidden subgroup
  257: \[
  258: K_i=\operatorname{Im}(\mathrm{ev}_{\omega_{v_i}})
  259: \]
  260: is generated by at most $\beta$ elements. The equalities
  261: \[
  262: K_1\cap K_2=0,
  263: \qquad
  264: K_1+K_2=G
  265: \]
  266: give an internal direct-sum decomposition
  267: \[
  268: G\cong K_1\oplus K_2
  269: \]
  270: with both summands nonzero. Hence $G$ is decomposable, and the finite abelian indecomposable groups are exactly the cyclic $p$-groups \cite[Chap.~12]{DummitFoote2004}. Moreover
  271: \[
  272: d(G)\le d(K_1)+d(K_2)\le 2\beta.
  273: \]
  274: Thus \textup{(i)} holds.
  275: 
  276: Finally, the exact-sequence consequences are separate from the realization step. The identities
  277: \[
  278: \bigcap_{i=1}^2 K_{v_i}=0,
  279: \qquad
  280: \sum_{i=1}^2 K_{v_i}=G
  281: \]
  282: give
  283: \[
  284: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{br}}(r)\cong B_{\mathrm{res}}(r)\cong G,
  285: \qquad
  286: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{unif}}(r)=0
  287: \]
  288: by \Cref{thm:branch-sensitive-visible-quotient,thm:branch-uniform-visible-quotient,thm:branch-comparison-exact-sequence}. Since both branches are non-neutral, \Cref{thm:mixed-branch-realization}(iv) gives
  289: \[
  290: M,p\Vdash \Null_s^{\mathrm{glue}}(r).
  291: \]
  292: \end{proof}
  293: 
  294: \begin{example}[A two-branch sphere example separating the aggregate invariants]\label{ex:two-branch-sphere-separation}
  295: This is the case $\beta=1$ and $G=(\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z)^2$ of \Cref{thm:bouquet-two-branch-resolution}.
  296: 
  297: Let $\mathcal U$ be a finite good cover of $S^2$ with nerve
  298: \[
  299: N:=N(\mathcal U),
  300: \]
  301: and take
  302: \[
  303: A=(\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z)^2.
  304: \]
  305: Since
  306: \[
  307: H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0,
  308: \qquad
  309: H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong \mathbb Z
  310: \]
  311: \cite[Secs.~2.1, 2.2]{Hatcher2002}, the universal coefficient theorem gives
  312: \[
  313: H^2(N,A)\cong \mathrm{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A)\cong A.
  314: \]
  315: Choose simplicial cocycles
  316: \[
  317: \alpha_1,\alpha_2\in Z^2(N,A)
  318: \]
  319: whose cohomology classes evaluate on the fundamental class $[S^2]$ as the basis vectors $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$. Define the local object functor
  320: \[
  321: F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}:\mathcal C_a^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
  322: \]
  323: by
  324: \[
  325: F_{p,s}(U)=\{v_1,v_2\}
  326: \]
  327: for every nonempty proper finite intersection $U$ of members of $\mathcal U$, by
  328: \[
  329: F_{p,s}(a)=\varnothing,
  330: \]
  331: and by the identity map on the labels $v_1,v_2$ under restriction. This local
  332: object functor and the prestack data below are used in the generated-subsite
  333: admissibility convention of \Cref{lem:generated-subsite-model-interface}. Then
  334: \[
  335: M,p\Vdash \CompSec_s(r),
  336: \qquad
  337: M,p\nVdash \Sec_s(r),
  338: \]
  339: and the sheafification has exactly two global visible classes, represented by the constant families $v_1$ and $v_2$ on the cover. For $m=1,2$, choose local realizations $x_i^{(m)}$ over the members of $\mathcal U$ with automorphism group $A$ and transition maps whose triple-overlap defect is $\alpha_m$. This is the two-label instance of the cocycle-to-prestack construction isolated in \Cref{lem:twisted-branch-prestack-model}; equivalently, it is the standard local-trivialization construction for gerbes \cite[Chap.~IV]{Giraud1971}, \cite[Sec.~5]{Breen2006}, \cite{Murray1996}. It yields a prestack presentation $\mathfrak P^{(m)}$ with
  340: \[
  341: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P^{(m)})
  342: \]
  343: equal to the singleton subpresheaf of $F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}$ generated by $v_m$, and its stackification
  344: \[
  345: \mathfrak G_{v_m}:=a(\mathfrak P^{(m)})
  346: \]
  347: is the corresponding non-neutral $A$-banded branch gerbe.
  348: 
  349: Form the disjoint union prestack
  350: \[
  351: \mathfrak P_r:=\mathfrak P^{(1)}\sqcup \mathfrak P^{(2)}
  352: \]
  353: and let
  354: \[
  355: \mathfrak L_r:=a(\mathfrak P_r).
  356: \]
  357: Its presheaf of isomorphism classes is the disjoint union of the two singleton local-object subpresheaves supplied by $\mathfrak P^{(1)}$ and $\mathfrak P^{(2)}$, hence
  358: \[
  359: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)\cong F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}.
  360: \]
  361: Therefore
  362: \[
  363: \Vis_{p,s}(r)=\{v_1,v_2\},
  364: \]
  365: while both component gerbes are non-neutral. Thus the two visible branches are recovered from explicit local-object data plus chosen local realization data, rather than inserted by taking gerbes as primitive input.
  366: 
  367: If $[S^2]$ denotes the fundamental class in $H_2(N,\mathbb Z)$, then
  368: \[
  369: \mathrm{ev}_{\omega_1}([S^2])=(1,0),
  370: \qquad
  371: \mathrm{ev}_{\omega_2}([S^2])=(0,1).
  372: \]
  373: Hence
  374: \[
  375: K_{v_1}=\langle(1,0)\rangle,
  376: \qquad
  377: K_{v_2}=\langle(0,1)\rangle,
  378: \qquad
  379: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{v_1}\cong A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{v_2}\cong \mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z.
  380: \]
  381: Therefore
  382: \[
  383: \bigcap_{v} K_v=0,
  384: \qquad
  385: \sum_{v} K_v=A,
  386: \qquad
  387: \max_v |K_v|=2.
  388: \]
  389: By \Cref{thm:branch-sensitive-visible-quotient,thm:branch-uniform-visible-quotient,thm:multi-branch-strictification-budget},
  390: \[
  391: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{br}}(r)\cong A,
  392: \qquad
  393: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{unif}}(r)=0,
  394: \qquad
  395: \log_2\!\bigl(\max_v |K_v|\bigr)=1.
  396: \]
  397: Moreover,
  398: \[
  399: B_{\mathrm{res}}(r)\cong A,
  400: \qquad
  401: \log_2|B_{\mathrm{res}}(r)|=2,
  402: \qquad
  403: \left|\bigcap_v K_v\right|=1.
  404: \]
  405: Thus each branch individually hides one coordinate of $A$, but the hidden coordinates are transverse: once the branch label is known, nothing remains hidden across all branches, whereas any single character channel admissible on both branches must be trivial. In this example the entire uniform hidden budget is exhausted by branch resolution itself.
  406: \end{example}
  407: 

 succeeded in 212ms:
    1: \begin{theorem}[Admissible-expansion mixed realization of neutral and obstructed branches on a formal \v{C}ech site]\label{thm:mixed-branch-realization}
    2: Let
    3: \[
    4: \mathcal U=\{U_i\to a\}_{i\in I}
    5: \]
    6: be a finite good-cover presentation in a pullback-based slice site $\mathcal C_a$ whose nonempty \v{C}ech nerve
    7: \[
    8: N:=N(\mathcal U)
    9: \]
   10: is connected. Equivalently, the realization of the formal cover is a
   11: paracompact space homotopy equivalent to $|N|$ and all nonempty finite
   12: intersections of members of $\mathcal U$ are acyclic for constant
   13: coefficients. Let $\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}$ be the formal \v{C}ech site of
   14: \Cref{lem:generated-subsite-model-interface}; it contains the strict
   15: initial object $\emptyset$ for empty intersections, and the \v{C}ech
   16: cochain complex below omits those pullback objects only because
   17: $A_{\mathcal U}(\emptyset)=0$. Let $A$ be a finite abelian group, and let
   18: $A_{\mathcal U}$ denote the genuine constant sheaf on the formal
   19: \v{C}ech site $\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}$ whose value on each nonempty
   20: basic intersection is identified with $A$. View $A_{\mathcal U}$ as the
   21: automorphism band of the realization prestack constructed below, and
   22: assume
   23: \[
   24: H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0.
   25: \]
   26: Use the Leray comparison isomorphism of
   27: \Cref{lem:generated-subsite-model-interface}(iv). The admissible-expansion
   28: realization conclusion below is relative to the deliberately broad
   29: well-typed admissible-expansion semantics of
   30: \Cref{prop:admissible-layer-expansion-interface}. Let $\Lambda$ be a finite nonempty set, and let
   31: \[
   32: \phi_\lambda:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\to A
   33: \]
   34: be a homomorphism for each $\lambda\in\Lambda$. Define
   35: \[
   36: \Lambda_0:=\{\lambda\in\Lambda\mid \phi_\lambda=0\}.
   37: \]
   38: Then, in the admissible-expansion model class generated by
   39: \Cref{prop:admissible-layer-expansion-interface}, the following typed data
   40: define a one-state $\mathbb L_2/\mathbb L_3$ model \(M\): the one-state
   41: \(\mathbb L_1\)-reduct, the formal site \(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}\), the
   42: local object functor \(F_{p,s}\) constructed below, and the realization
   43: prestack
   44: \[
   45: \mathfrak P_r\to \mathcal C_{\mathcal U}
   46: \]
   47: with band \(A_{\mathcal U}\). In that generated model there are a state
   48: \(p\) and a distinguished admitted reference \(r:\mathsf{Ref}_s\) with
   49: \([r]_p=a\), local slice site \(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}\), and
   50: \(\mathfrak L_r:=a(\mathfrak P_r)\), such that the following hold.
   51: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
   52: \item
   53: \[
   54: \Vis_{p,s}(r)\cong \Lambda.
   55: \]
   56: \item Under the identification in \textup{(i)}, for each $\lambda\in\Lambda$ the corresponding component gerbe $\mathfrak G_\lambda\subseteq \mathfrak L_r$ has branch obstruction class
   57: \[
   58: \omega_\lambda\in H^2(N,A)
   59: \]
   60: satisfying
   61: \[
   62: \mathrm{ev}_{\omega_\lambda}=\phi_\lambda,
   63: \qquad
   64: K_\lambda=\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda).
   65: \]
   66: Consequently,
   67: \[
   68: \mathfrak G_\lambda\ \text{is neutral}
   69: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   70: \phi_\lambda=0.
   71: \]
   72: \item $\mathfrak P_r$ is globally conservative at $a$. More precisely, if $\Lambda_0=\varnothing$ this is the vacuous terminal-fibre case
   73: \[
   74: \mathfrak P_r(a)=\varnothing=\mathfrak L_r(a),
   75: \]
   76: while if $\Lambda_0\neq\varnothing$ the terminal objects of $\mathfrak L_r(a)$ lie exactly in the neutral components indexed by $\Lambda_0$ and are in the essential image of $\mathfrak P_r(a)$. In either case
   77: \[
   78: M,p\Vdash \Sec_s(r)
   79: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   80: \Lambda_0\neq\varnothing.
   81: \]
   82: \item
   83: \[
   84: M,p\Vdash \CompSec_s(r),
   85: \]
   86: and
   87: \[
   88: M,p\Vdash \Null^{\mathrm{glue}}_s(r)
   89: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
   90: \Lambda_0=\varnothing.
   91: \]
   92: \item The aggregate visibility quotients satisfy
   93: \[
   94: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{br}}(r)\cong A\Big/\bigcap_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda),
   95: \qquad
   96: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{unif}}(r)\cong A\Big/\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda).
   97: \]
   98: \end{enumerate}
   99: \end{theorem}
  100: 
  101: \begin{proof}
  102: Since
  103: \[
  104: H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0,
  105: \]
  106: the universal coefficient sequence reduces to an isomorphism
  107: \[
  108: \eta_A:H^2(N,A)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Hom}(H_2(N,\mathbb Z),A).
  109: \]
  110: For each $\lambda\in\Lambda$, let
  111: \[
  112: \omega_\lambda\in H^2(N,A)
  113: \]
  114: be the unique class satisfying
  115: \[
  116: \eta_A(\omega_\lambda)=\phi_\lambda,
  117: \]
  118: and choose the normalized representative as follows. If
  119: \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\), set
  120: \[
  121: \alpha_\lambda:=0\in Z^2(N,A).
  122: \]
  123: If \(\lambda\notin\Lambda_0\), choose any normalized simplicial cocycle
  124: \[
  125: \alpha_\lambda\in Z^2(N,A)
  126: \]
  127: representing \(\omega_\lambda\), with the alternating ordered-triple
  128: extension of \Cref{lem:twisted-branch-prestack-model}. Thus
  129: \[
  130: \lambda\in\Lambda_0
  131: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  132: \omega_\lambda=0,
  133: \]
  134: and, by the normalization just stipulated rather than by an arbitrary choice
  135: of representative, the labels in \(\Lambda_0\) are exactly the labels for
  136: which the construction uses the zero cocycle \(\alpha_\lambda=0\). For
  137: \(\lambda\notin\Lambda_0\) the chosen class is nonzero, so any representing
  138: cocycle is nonzero. No assertion is made that every normalized cocycle
  139: representing the zero cohomology class is itself zero.
  140: 
  141: By \Cref{lem:generated-subsite-model-interface}(i)--(iv), pass to the
  142: formal \v{C}ech site $\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}$ appearing in the statement.
  143: The cover $\mathcal U$, its nerve, and the constant-band finite cochain
  144: data are the same, and Leray comparison identifies
  145: $H^2(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U},A_{\mathcal U})$ with $H^2(N,A)$. The derived branch class
  146: used below is therefore the actual derived class corresponding to the
  147: chosen finite-nerve cocycle.
  148: 
  149: Define the local object functor
  150: \[
  151: F_{p,s}:\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}^{op}\to\mathsf{Set}
  152: \]
  153: by
  154: \[
  155: F_{p,s}(U):=\Lambda
  156: \qquad
  157: \text{for every nonempty proper intersection object }U\to a,
  158: \]
  159: \[
  160: F_{p,s}(a):=\Lambda_0,
  161: \]
  162: and
  163: \[
  164: F_{p,s}(\emptyset):=\{\ast\}.
  165: \]
  166: Restrictions between nonempty proper intersection objects are identities,
  167: the restriction maps
  168: \[
  169: F_{p,s}(a)\to F_{p,s}(U)
  170: \]
  171: are the inclusions $\Lambda_0\hookrightarrow \Lambda$, and every
  172: restriction to $\emptyset$ is the unique map.
  173: 
  174: This is the presheaf \(F_{\Lambda_0}\) of
  175: \Cref{lem:formal-label-sheafification}. That lemma uses the plus--plus
  176: description of sheafification and the strict initial object for empty
  177: pullbacks, so no one-step matching-family description is being assumed.
  178: Since the nonempty nerve is connected, it gives
  179: \[
  180: \bigl(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\bigr)(a)\cong \Lambda.
  181: \]
  182: 
  183: For each $\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_0$, define a category fibred in groupoids
  184: \[
  185: \mathfrak P_\lambda\to \mathcal C_{\mathcal U}
  186: \]
  187: exactly as in the proof of \Cref{thm:prescribed-branch-realization}: on the terminal fibre set
  188: \[
  189: \mathfrak P_\lambda(a):=\varnothing,
  190: \]
  191: and on each nonempty proper formal intersection object $W$, with
  192: \[
  193: I(W):=\{i\in I\mid W\to U_i\},
  194: \]
  195: let the fibre $\mathfrak P_\lambda(W)$ have object set
  196: \[
  197: \{x_i^{(\lambda,W)}\mid i\in I(W)\},
  198: \]
  199: morphism sets equal to \(A_{\mathcal U}(W)\), identified with \(A\) on
  200: connected nonempty \(W\), and composition twisted by the cocycle
  201: \(\alpha_\lambda\) under this identification. Over $\emptyset$ take the
  202: terminal groupoid, with unique restrictions to it. The argument of
  203: \Cref{thm:prescribed-branch-realization} shows that $\mathfrak P_\lambda$
  204: is a prestack with genuine constant band $A_{\mathcal U}$, that
  205: \[
  206: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_\lambda)(U)\cong\{ \lambda\}
  207: \]
  208: for every nonempty proper intersection object $U\to a$, that
  209: \[
  210: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_\lambda)(a)=\varnothing,
  211: \]
  212: that the fibre over $\emptyset$ is terminal, and that the \v{C}ech cocycle
  213: attached to the branch is exactly $\alpha_\lambda$. By
  214: \Cref{lem:formal-site-constant-sheaf-banding}, the corresponding
  215: isomorphism and automorphism presheaves on every slice are restrictions of
  216: the genuine constant sheaf $A_{\mathcal U}$, so no constant-presheaf
  217: sheaf condition is being assumed.
  218: 
  219: For each $\lambda\in\Lambda_0$, define a stack in groupoids
  220: \[
  221: \mathfrak P_\lambda\to \mathcal C_{\mathcal U}
  222: \]
  223: by taking, for every nonempty object $U$ of $\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}$, a one-object groupoid with distinguished object $y_U^{(\lambda)}$ and automorphism group $A_{\mathcal U}(U)$, by taking the terminal groupoid over $\emptyset$, and by taking every restriction between nonempty objects to be the restriction map of the genuine constant sheaf on the distinguished object and on automorphisms. Restrictions to $\emptyset$ are unique. By \Cref{lem:formal-site-neutral-stack-h1}, this neutral one-object prestack is a stack with genuine constant band $A_{\mathcal U}$: object descent is governed by \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-torsor cocycles on slices, and those torsors are trivial because \(H^1(\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}/W,A_{\mathcal U})=0\) for every relevant object \(W\). Thus
  224: \[
  225: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_\lambda)(U)\cong\{\lambda\}
  226: \]
  227: for every nonempty object $U$ of $\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}$, has terminal component set over $\emptyset$, and the \v{C}ech cocycle on the cover $\mathcal U$ is identically zero.
  228: 
  229: Now set
  230: \[
  231: \mathfrak P_r:=\coprod_{\lambda\in\Lambda}\mathfrak P_\lambda.
  232: \]
  233: As in \Cref{lem:twisted-branch-prestack-model}, this is the fibrewise
  234: coproduct over nonempty objects with the shared terminal fibre over
  235: $\emptyset$.
  236: By \Cref{lem:twisted-branch-prestack-model}, this coproduct is a prestack with genuine constant band $A_{\mathcal U}$: the nonzero summands are the twisted groupoids determined by the chosen representatives $\alpha_\lambda$, the neutral summands are indexed by those \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\) for which we explicitly set \(\alpha_\lambda=0\), the restriction maps are strictly functorial, and the Cech cocycle of the $\lambda$-component is the chosen $\alpha_\lambda$. Since distinct summands have no morphisms between them, its presheaf of isomorphism classes is
  237: \[
  238: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)(U)\cong \Lambda
  239: \qquad
  240: \text{for every nonempty proper intersection object }U\to a,
  241: \]
  242: and
  243: \[
  244: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)(a)\cong \Lambda_0.
  245: \]
  246: Over $\emptyset$ the shared terminal fibre gives
  247: $\pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)(\emptyset)\cong\{\ast\}=F_{p,s}(\emptyset)$.
  248: Hence
  249: \[
  250: \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)\cong F_{p,s}.
  251: \]
  252: By \Cref{lem:generated-subsite-model-interface}(v)--(vi), the functor
  253: $F_{p,s}$ and the prestack $\mathfrak P_r$ determine a one-state semantic
  254: model $M$ with admitted reference $r:\mathsf{Ref}_s$ at its unique state $p$,
  255: with reference class $[r]_p=a$, local slice site $\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}$,
  256: local object functor $F_{p,s}$, and realization prestack $\mathfrak P_r$ with
  257: genuine constant band $A_{\mathcal U}$. This is again the admissible one-state
  258: $\mathbb L_2/\mathbb L_3$ expansion supplied by
  259: \Cref{prop:admissible-layer-expansion-interface}, and no independently
  260: restricted semantic model class is being used.
  261: The construction has now supplied the same interfaces as in the prescribed case: admitted reference $r$, formal \v{C}ech site $\mathcal C_{\mathcal U}$, local object functor $F_{p,s}$, genuine constant sheaf band $A_{\mathcal U}$, the Leray comparison isomorphism for the cover $\mathcal U$, visible classes obtained from sheafified terminal sections, and forcing clauses determined by compatible local sections and actual terminal sections.
  262: Applying \Cref{prop:stackification-components} gives
  263: \[
  264: \pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)\cong a_{p,s}F_{p,s},
  265: \]
  266: so
  267: \[
  268: \Vis_{p,s}(r)\cong \bigl(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\bigr)(a)\cong \Lambda.
  269: \]
  270: This proves \textup{(i)}.
  271: 
  272: For each $\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\Lambda_0$, the same calculation as in \Cref{thm:prescribed-branch-realization} shows that the component gerbe $\mathfrak G_\lambda$ has branch obstruction class
  273: \[
  274: \omega_\lambda=[\alpha_\lambda]
  275: \]
  276: and therefore
  277: \[
  278: \mathrm{ev}_{\omega_\lambda}=\eta_A(\omega_\lambda)=\phi_\lambda,
  279: \qquad
  280: K_\lambda=\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda).
  281: \]
  282: For these nonzero branches the finite-nerve comparison again uses the same good cover $\mathcal U$, the constant band $A$, and the Leray comparison class of the normalized cocycle $\alpha_\lambda$.
  283: For each $\lambda\in\Lambda_0$, \Cref{lem:formal-site-neutral-stack-h1}
  284: shows that the neutral one-object construction is a genuine neutral
  285: \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-banded stack on every slice. Choosing the objects
  286: $y_{U_i}^{(\lambda)}$ together with the identity isomorphisms on overlaps
  287: yields the zero \v{C}ech $2$-cocycle. Hence
  288: \[
  289: \omega_\lambda=0,
  290: \qquad
  291: \mathrm{ev}_{\omega_\lambda}=0=\phi_\lambda,
  292: \qquad
  293: K_\lambda=0=\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda).
  294: \]
  295: The neutrality criterion in \Cref{prop:derived-branch-classification} therefore gives
  296: \[
  297: \mathfrak G_\lambda\ \text{is neutral}
  298: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  299: \omega_\lambda=0
  300: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  301: \phi_\lambda=0.
  302: \]
  303: This proves \textup{(ii)}.
  304: 
  305: The identity
  306: \[
  307: F_{p,s}(a)=\Lambda_0
  308: \]
  309: immediately gives
  310: \[
  311: M,p\Vdash \Sec_s(r)
  312: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  313: \Lambda_0\neq\varnothing.
  314: \]
  315: It remains to prove global conservativity at $a$. If \(\lambda\in\Lambda_0\),
  316: the preceding paragraph and \Cref{lem:formal-site-neutral-stack-h1} identify
  317: \(\mathfrak P_\lambda\) with its own stackification on the terminal slice:
  318: every object descent datum has a trivial \(A_{\mathcal U}\)-torsor class, and
  319: morphism descent is the sheaf condition for \(A_{\mathcal U}\). Hence the
  320: restriction of the stackification morphism to the fibre over \(a\) is an
  321: equivalence on that summand. If $\lambda\notin\Lambda_0$, then
  322: $\phi_\lambda\neq 0$, hence $\omega_\lambda\neq 0$, and
  323: \Cref{prop:derived-branch-classification} implies that the component gerbe
  324: $\mathfrak G_\lambda$ is non-neutral. Therefore
  325: \[
  326: \mathfrak G_\lambda(a)=\varnothing
  327: \qquad
  328: (\lambda\notin\Lambda_0)
  329: \]
  330: by \Cref{lem:terminal-fibre-neutrality}. Consequently every object of $\mathfrak L_r(a)$ lies in a zero-branch component and is therefore in the essential image of $\mathfrak P_r(a)$. Thus $\mathfrak P_r$ is globally conservative at $a$. This proves \textup{(iii)}.
  331: 
  332: Since $\Lambda\neq\varnothing$, the identification
  333: \[
  334: \bigl(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\bigr)(a)\cong \Lambda
  335: \]
  336: shows that
  337: \[
  338: \bigl(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\bigr)(a)\neq\varnothing.
  339: \]
  340: Choose any $\lambda\in\Lambda$ and assign the same section $\lambda$ to
  341: every member $U_i$ of the cover $\mathcal U$. The restriction maps between
  342: nonempty proper intersections are identities and all restrictions to
  343: $\emptyset$ are unique, so this is a strict matching family on
  344: $\mathcal U$. Therefore \Cref{def:existence-levels}(ii) gives
  345: \[
  346: M,p\Vdash \CompSec_s(r).
  347: \]
  348: Using \textup{(iii)} and the definition
  349: \[
  350: \Null^{\mathrm{glue}}_s(r):=\Adm_s(r)\land \CompSec_s(r)\land \neg\Sec_s(r),
  351: \]
  352: one gets
  353: \[
  354: M,p\Vdash \Null^{\mathrm{glue}}_s(r)
  355: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  356: \Lambda_0=\varnothing.
  357: \]
  358: This proves \textup{(iv)}.
  359: 
  360: Finally, \textup{(v)} is \Cref{thm:branch-sensitive-visible-quotient,thm:branch-uniform-visible-quotient} applied to the family
  361: \[
  362: K_\lambda=\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda)
  363: \qquad
  364: (\lambda\in\Lambda).
  365: \qedhere
  366: \]
  367: \end{proof}
  368: 
  369: \begin{corollary}[Exact subgroup classification on torsion-free second homology]\label{cor:torsion-free-h2-classification}
  370: Assume the full hypothesis package of \Cref{thm:mixed-branch-realization}: a
  371: fixed finite nerve $N=N(\mathcal U)$ in a formal \v{C}ech good-cover site,
  372: a trivialized constant finite abelian band $A$, the Leray comparison
  373: isomorphism for the finite-nerve classes, the constructed realization
  374: prestack, and global conservativity at the terminal fibre. Assume
  375: in addition that
  376: \[
  377: H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong \mathbb Z^\beta
  378: \]
  379: for some integer $\beta\ge 0$. Let $(K_\lambda)_{\lambda\in\Lambda}$ be a family of subgroups of $A$. Then the following are equivalent.
  380: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  381: \item There exist a state $p$, an admitted reference $r:\mathsf{Ref}_s$ on the fixed nerve $N$, and a bijection
  382: \[
  383: \Vis_{p,s}(r)\cong \Lambda
  384: \]
  385: such that the branch indexed by $\lambda$ has finite-nerve-class hidden subgroup
  386: \[
  387: K_\lambda.
  388: \]
  389: \item Every subgroup $K_\lambda$ is generated by at most $\beta$ elements.
  390: \end{enumerate}
  391: When these conditions hold,
  392: \[
  393: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{br}}(r)\cong A\Big/\bigcap_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K_\lambda,
  394: \qquad
  395: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\mathrm{unif}}(r)\cong A\Big/\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda}K_\lambda,
  396: \]
  397: and
  398: \[
  399: M,p\Vdash \Sec_s(r)
  400: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  401: K_\lambda=0\ \text{for some}\ \lambda\in\Lambda.
  402: \]
  403: In particular, a single branch realizes maximal collapse
  404: \[
  405: K_\omega=A
  406: \]
  407: if and only if $A$ is generated by at most $\beta$ elements. For $\beta=1$, the realizable single-branch finite-nerve-class hidden subgroups are exactly the cyclic subgroups of $A$.
  408: \end{corollary}
  409: 
  410: \begin{proof}
  411: Assume \textup{(i)}. For any branch on the fixed finite nerve satisfying the
  412: finite-nerve comparison hypotheses, its finite-nerve-class hidden subgroup is by
  413: definition the image of the homological evaluation map attached to its branch
  414: obstruction class. Since
  415: \[
  416: H_1(N,\mathbb Z)=0,
  417: \]
  418: the universal coefficient comparison identifies that evaluation map with a
  419: homomorphism from $H_2(N,\mathbb Z)$ to $A$. Thus, for each $\lambda$, there
  420: exists a homomorphism
  421: \[
  422: \phi_\lambda:H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\to A
  423: \]
  424: with
  425: \[
  426: K_\lambda=\operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda).
  427: \]
  428: After choosing an identification
  429: \[
  430: H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong \mathbb Z^\beta,
  431: \]
  432: the image of $\phi_\lambda$ is generated by the images of the $\beta$ standard basis vectors. Hence $K_\lambda$ is generated by at most $\beta$ elements. Therefore \textup{(ii)} holds.
  433: 
  434: Assume conversely that \textup{(ii)} holds. For each $\lambda\in\Lambda$, choose generators
  435: \[
  436: k_{\lambda,1},\dots,k_{\lambda,\beta}\in K_\lambda
  437: \]
  438: of $K_\lambda$, allowing repetitions and zeros when fewer than $\beta$ generators are needed. Define
  439: \[
  440: \phi_\lambda:\mathbb Z^\beta\to A,
  441: \qquad
  442: (n_1,\dots,n_\beta)\longmapsto \sum_{j=1}^{\beta} n_j k_{\lambda,j}.
  443: \]
  444: Then
  445: \[
  446: \operatorname{Im}(\phi_\lambda)=K_\lambda.
  447: \]
  448: Transporting these maps across the chosen isomorphism
  449: \[
  450: H_2(N,\mathbb Z)\cong \mathbb Z^\beta
  451: \]
