Therefore $\mathcal I_{\sigma,x}$ is a $\Band$-torsor. This is the usual Isom-torsor construction for a banded stack \cite[Chap.~III]{MacLaneMoerdijk1994}, \cite[Tag 04TU]{StacksProject}.
  893: 
  894: The nonabelian cohomology set $H^1(\mathcal C_a,\Band)$ classifies $\Band$-torsors on $\mathcal C_a$ up to isomorphism. Since $\Band$ is here an abelian sheaf, the distinguished point $0\in H^1(\mathcal C_a,\Band)$ is the class of the trivial torsor. If $H^1(\mathcal C_a,\Band)=0$, every $\Band$-torsor is trivial; hence $\mathcal I_{\sigma,x}$ is isomorphic to the trivial torsor $\Band$ and has a global section. Such a section is exactly an isomorphism $\iota_r(y)\cong x$ in the terminal fibre $\mathfrak L_r(a)$.
  895: \end{proof}
  896: 
  897: \begin{lemma}[Realizing component sections by descent]\label{lem:component-section-descent-object}
  898: Let $\mathfrak P_r$ be a representative-rigid realization prestack on
  899: $\mathcal C_a$, assume that $F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}$ is
  900: slice-separated, and let
  901: \[
  902: \iota_r:\mathfrak P_r\to\mathfrak L_r:=a(\mathfrak P_r)
  903: \]
  904: be the stackification map. Every section
  905: \[
  906: \xi\in\pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)(a)
  907: \]
  908: is represented by an object of the terminal stack fibre $\mathfrak L_r(a)$.
  909: \end{lemma}
  910: 
  911: \begin{proof}
  912: Let
  913: \[
  914: F:=F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}.
  915: \]
  916: The identification
  917: \[
  918: \pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)\cong a_{p,s}F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}
  919: \]
  920: from \Cref{prop:stackification-components} writes $\xi$ as a sheafified
  921: matching datum. We now use the strictified plus--plus normal form
  922: explicitly, because a section of \(aF\) is not, for a general presheaf, a
  923: strict \(F\)-matching family on the first displayed cover. By
  924: \Cref{lem:strictify-component-representatives}, applied in the slice
  925: \(\mathcal C_a\), there is a
  926: covering family
  927: \[
  928: \{U_i\to a\}_{i\in I}
  929: \]
  930: and sections
  931: \[
  932: \sigma_i\in F_{p,s}(U_i)
  933: \]
  934: such that the sheaf sections \(u_{U_i}(\sigma_i)\) glue to \(\xi\), and
  935: such that for every pair \(i,j\), with
  936: \[
  937: U_{ij}:=U_i\times_a U_j,
  938: \]
  939: the two restrictions agree on the original overlap:
  940: \[
  941: \res_{U_{ij}}^{U_i}(\sigma_i)
  942: =
  943: \res_{U_{ij}}^{U_j}(\sigma_j)
  944: \qquad(\forall i,j),
  945: \]
  946: including the case where \(U_{ij}\) is the formal initial object
  947: representing an empty overlap. The cited strictification lemma is the
  948: point at which slice-separatedness, rather than separatedness only at
  949: \(a\), is needed: the plus--plus representative first gives equality only
  950: after covers of the \(U_{ij}\), and separatedness at those overlap objects
  951: upgrades it to equality on the original \(U_{ij}\).
  952: Representative-rigidity supplies strict representatives
  953: \[
  954: y_i:=s_{U_i}(\sigma_i)\in\mathfrak P_r(U_i).
  955: \]
  956: On every overlap, strict functoriality gives equality of the restricted
  957: representatives
  958: \[
  959: y_i|_{U_{ij}}=s_{U_{ij}}(\sigma_i|_{U_{ij}})
  960: =s_{U_{ij}}(\sigma_j|_{U_{ij}})=y_j|_{U_{ij}}.
  961: \]
  962: These equalities are equalities in the fibre over the actual pullback
  963: object \(U_{ij}\). They are not merely equalities after a further cover of
  964: \(U_{ij}\). This is why the following identity arrows are legitimate
  965: cover-level descent arrows; without the preceding slice-separated
  966: strictification, identity descent data on the displayed cover would not be
  967: defined.
  968: Taking the identity isomorphism of this common object on each
  969: $U_{ij}$ therefore gives the overlap arrow
  970: \[
  971: \theta_{ij}:y_i|_{U_{ij}}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}}y_j|_{U_{ij}}
  972: \]
  973: inside $\mathfrak L_r(U_{ij})$. On a triple overlap
  974: $U_{ijk}:=U_i\times_aU_j\times_aU_k$, the two composites
  975: \[
  976: \theta_{ik}|_{U_{ijk}},
  977: \qquad
  978: \theta_{jk}|_{U_{ijk}}\circ\theta_{ij}|_{U_{ijk}}
  979: \]
  980: are both the identity of the same restricted object, including when this
  981: triple overlap is the formal initial object. Thus the cocycle condition
  982: for descent is satisfied strictly. Since $\mathfrak L_r$ is a stack,
  983: this descent datum glues to an object
  984: \[
  985: x_\xi\in\mathfrak L_r(a).
  986: \]
  987: By construction, the connected-component class of $x_\xi$ is the original
  988: section $\xi$.
  989: \end{proof}
  990: 
  991: \begin{theorem}[Rigidity of representative-rigid lifts over slice-separated slices]\label{thm:canonical-lift-rigidity}
  992: Let $r:\mathsf{Ref}_s$ be admitted at a state $p$, write
  993: \[
  994: a=[r]_p,
  995: \qquad
  996: \mathcal C_a:=\mathcal C_{p,s}/a,
  997: \]
  998: and let
  999: \[
 1000: \mathfrak P_r\to \mathcal C_a
 1001: \]
 1002: be a representative-rigid realization prestack with band $\Band$. Let
 1003: \[
 1004: u:F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}\longrightarrow a_{p,s}F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}
 1005: \]
 1006: be the sheafification unit. Assume throughout this theorem that
 1007: \[
 1008: F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}
 1009: \]
 1010: is slice-separated in the sense of
 1011: \Cref{def:slice-separated-local-object}; equivalently, $F_{p,s}$ is
 1012: separated at every object of the slice $\mathcal C_a$, including every
 1013: cover member, every pullback overlap, and every refinement occurring below.
 1014: Separatedness only at the terminal object $a$ is not sufficient for this
 1015: theorem, and no assertion below is made under that weaker hypothesis.
 1016: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
 1017: \item If $\mathfrak P_r$ is globally conservative at $a$, then
 1018: \[
 1019: u_a:F_{p,s}(a)\to \bigl(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\bigr)(a)
 1020: \]
 1021: is surjective.
 1022: \item If
 1023: \[
 1024: H^1(\mathcal C_a,\Band)=0,
 1025: \]
 1026: then surjectivity of $u_a$ implies that $\mathfrak P_r$ is globally
 1027: conservative at $a$.
 1028: \end{enumerate}
 1029: Under the same vanishing hypothesis the following are equivalent:
 1030: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),nosep]
 1031: \item $\mathfrak P_r$ is globally conservative at $a$;
 1032: \item $u_a$ is bijective;
 1033: \item $F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}$ satisfies the sheaf gluing condition at
 1034: the object $a$, that is, every matching family on every cover of $a$ has
 1035: an amalgamation in $F_{p,s}(a)$; uniqueness follows from the standing
 1036: slice-separatedness at $a$.
 1037: \end{enumerate}
 1038: The last equivalence is not a consequence of separatedness at \(a\) alone.
 1039: It uses separatedness at all cover members, pullback overlaps, and their
 1040: refinements in the slice \(\mathcal C_a\).
 1041: \end{theorem}
 1042: 
 1043: \begin{proof}
 1044: Write
 1045: \[
 1046: \mathfrak L_r:=a(\mathfrak P_r).
 1047: \]
 1048: By \Cref{prop:stackification-component-unit-naturality}, the morphism of
 1049: component sheaves induced by the stackification map
 1050: \[
 1051: \iota_r:\mathfrak P_r\to \mathfrak L_r
 1052: \]
 1053: identifies with the sheafification unit
 1054: \[
 1055: u:F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}\to a_{p,s}F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}.
 1056: \]
 1057: 
 1058: Assume first that $\mathfrak P_r$ is globally conservative at $a$, and let
 1059: \[
 1060: \xi\in \bigl(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\bigr)(a)\cong \pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)(a).
 1061: \]
 1062: By \Cref{lem:component-section-descent-object}, choose an object
 1063: \[
 1064: x\in \mathfrak L_r(a)
 1065: \]
 1066: whose connected-component class is $\xi$. The cited lemma uses the
 1067: plus--plus normal form of \Cref{lem:strict-sheafification-representatives}:
 1068: first one represents $\xi$ by $F_{p,s}$-sections whose overlap equalities
 1069: hold after local refinements, then slice-separatedness upgrades those
 1070: refined overlap equalities to a strict matching family, and only then does
 1071: effective descent in the stackification produce the terminal object.
 1072: Essential surjectivity of
 1073: \[
 1074: (\iota_r)_a:(\mathfrak P_r)_a\to (\mathfrak L_r)_a
 1075: \]
 1076: gives an object
 1077: \[
 1078: y\in \mathfrak P_r(a)
 1079: \]
 1080: with $\iota_r(y)\cong x$. Let
 1081: \[
 1082: \sigma:=[y]\in \pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)(a)\cong F_{p,s}(a).
 1083: \]
 1084: Since $u_a$ is the map on connected components induced by $\iota_r$, one has
 1085: \[
 1086: u_a(\sigma)=[\iota_r(y)]=[x]=\xi.
 1087: \]
 1088: Thus $u_a$ is surjective.
 1089: 
 1090: Assume next that $H^1(\mathcal C_a,\Band)=0$ and that $u_a$ is surjective. Let
 1091: \[
 1092: x\in \mathfrak L_r(a).
 1093: \]
 1094: Its connected-component class
 1095: \[
 1096: [x]\in \pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)(a)\cong \bigl(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\bigr)(a)
 1097: \]
 1098: lies in the image of $u_a$, so choose
 1099: \[
 1100: \sigma\in F_{p,s}(a)
 1101: \]
 1102: with
 1103: \[
 1104: u_a(\sigma)=[x].
 1105: \]
 1106: Put
 1107: \[
 1108: y:=s_a(\sigma)\in \mathfrak P_r(a).
 1109: \]
 1110: By construction, $y$ represents the class $\sigma$ in $\pi_0^{\mathrm{pre}}(\mathfrak P_r)(a)$, hence
 1111: \[
 1112: [\iota_r(y)]=u_a(\sigma)=[x]
 1113: \]
 1114: in $\pi_0(\mathfrak L_r)(a)$. By \Cref{lem:canonical-lift-isom-torsor}, the sheaf
 1115: \[
 1116: \underline{\mathrm{Isom}}_{\mathfrak L_r}(\iota_r(y),x)
 1117: \]
 1118: is a $\Band$-torsor, and the vanishing of $H^1(\mathcal C_a,\Band)$ gives a global section of it. Equivalently, there exists an isomorphism
 1119: \[
 1120: \iota_r(y)\xrightarrow{\sim} x
 1121: \]
 1122: in $\mathfrak L_r(a)$. Thus every object of $\mathfrak L_r(a)$ is in the essential image of $(\iota_r)_a$, so $\mathfrak P_r$ is globally conservative at $a$.
 1123: 
 1124: The standing slice-separatedness also gives injectivity of the unit at the
 1125: terminal object. If
 1126: \[
 1127: u_a(\sigma)=u_a(\tau)
 1128: \qquad
 1129: (\sigma,\tau\in F_{p,s}(a)),
 1130: \]
 1131: then the two global sections define the same element of the sheafification at $a$.
 1132: Applying \Cref{lem:sheafification-unit-equality-local} gives a cover
 1133: \[
 1134: \{U_i\to a\}_{i\in I}
 1135: \]
 1136: such that
 1137: \[
 1138: \res_{U_i}^a(\sigma)=\res_{U_i}^a(\tau)
 1139: \qquad
 1140: (\forall i\in I).
 1141: \]
 1142: Separatedness on the slice applies in particular at the terminal object
 1143: $a$, and therefore forces $\sigma=\tau$. Thus $u_a$ is injective.
 1144: 
 1145: Under $H^1(\mathcal C_a,\Band)=0$, part \textup{(i)} gives
 1146: \textup{(a)}$\Rightarrow$\textup{(b)}, and part \textup{(ii)} together
 1147: with injectivity gives \textup{(b)}$\Rightarrow$\textup{(a)}. It remains
 1148: to identify \textup{(b)} with the terminal sheaf axiom, and we spell this
 1149: out because this is the point where the slice-separated hypothesis is
 1150: used beyond the terminal object.
 1151: 
 1152: Assume \textup{(b)}, and let
 1153: \[
 1154: \{U_i\to a\}_{i\in I},
 1155: \qquad
 1156: \sigma_i\in F_{p,s}(U_i)
 1157: \]
 1158: be a strict matching family on a cover of \(a\). Applying \(u\) to the
 1159: \(\sigma_i\) gives a matching family in the sheaf
 1160: \(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\), hence a unique section
 1161: \[
 1162: \xi\in \bigl(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\bigr)(a)
 1163: \]
 1164: whose restriction to \(U_i\) is \(u_{U_i}(\sigma_i)\). By surjectivity of
 1165: \(u_a\), choose \(\sigma\in F_{p,s}(a)\) with \(u_a(\sigma)=\xi\). For
 1166: each \(i\),
 1167: \[
 1168: u_{U_i}\bigl(\res_{U_i}^{a}\sigma\bigr)=u_{U_i}(\sigma_i)
 1169: \]
 1170: in \((a_{p,s}F_{p,s})(U_i)\). This equality is not an equality in
 1171: \(F_{p,s}(U_i)\). By \Cref{lem:sheafification-unit-equality-local},
 1172: there is a covering family
 1173: \[
 1174: \{V_{ik}\to U_i\}_{k}
 1175: \]
 1176: such that
 1177: \[
 1178: \res_{V_{ik}}^{a}\sigma=\res_{V_{ik}}^{U_i}\sigma_i
 1179: \qquad(\forall k)
 1180: \]
 1181: in \(F_{p,s}(V_{ik})\). Since \(U_i\) is an object of the slice
 1182: \(\mathcal C_a\) and the standing hypothesis is slice-separatedness,
 1183: \(F_{p,s}\) is separated at \(U_i\). The displayed local equalities over
 1184: the cover of \(U_i\) therefore force
 1185: \[
 1186: \res_{U_i}^{a}\sigma=\sigma_i
 1187: \]
 1188: in \(F_{p,s}(U_i)\). Thus \(\sigma\) is an amalgamation of the original
 1189: matching family. Its uniqueness follows from separatedness at \(a\).
 1190: Therefore \textup{(c)} holds.
 1191: 
 1192: Conversely, assume \textup{(c)} and let
 1193: \[
 1194: \xi\in \bigl(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\bigr)(a).
 1195: \]
 1196: By the plus--plus normal form of
 1197: \Cref{lem:strict-sheafification-representatives}, \(\xi\) is represented
 1198: on a cover \(\{U_i\to a\}\) by sections
 1199: \(\sigma_i\in F_{p,s}(U_i)\) whose restrictions to each overlap
 1200: \[
 1201: U_{ij}:=U_i\times_aU_j
 1202: \]
 1203: agree after a further covering family of \(U_{ij}\). Each \(U_{ij}\) is an
 1204: object of the slice \(\mathcal C_a\), so slice-separatedness and
 1205: \Cref{lem:slice-separated-refined-equality} upgrade these refined
 1206: equalities to equalities on the original overlaps. Hence the
 1207: \(\sigma_i\) form a strict matching family on the displayed cover. By the
 1208: sheaf axiom at \(a\), there is an amalgamation
 1209: \(\sigma\in F_{p,s}(a)\). The sections \(u_a(\sigma)\) and \(\xi\) have
 1210: the same restrictions to the cover \(\{U_i\to a\}\), so separatedness of
 1211: the sheaf \(a_{p,s}F_{p,s}\) gives \(u_a(\sigma)=\xi\). Thus \(u_a\) is
 1212: surjective, and its injectivity was proved above. Therefore
 1213: \textup{(b)} holds.
 1214: \end{proof}
 1215: 
 1216: \begin{corollary}[Nontrivial gluing semantics forces noncanonical lift data]\label{cor:no-rigid-gluing-null}
 1217: Let $r:\mathsf{Ref}_s$ be admitted at a state $p$, write
 1218: \[
 1219: a=[r]_p,
 1220: \qquad
 1221: \mathcal C_a:=\mathcal C_{p,s}/a,
 1222: \]
 1223: assume that $F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}$ is slice-separated. If
 1224: \[
 1225: M,p\Vdash \Null^{\mathrm{glue}}_s(r),
 1226: \]
 1227: then no representative-rigid realization prestack with band $\Band$ can be globally conservative at $a$.
 1228: \end{corollary}
 1229: 
 1230: \begin{proof}
 1231: The condition
 1232: \[
 1233: M,p\Vdash \Null^{\mathrm{glue}}_s(r)
 1234: \]
 1235: means that $r$ is admitted, that

 succeeded in 236ms:
    1: \section{Homological visibility and finite-nerve-class quotients}
    2: \label{sec:homological-visibility}
    3: 
    4: We now fix one visible branch together with a finite nerve presentation. The quotient attached to a chosen cocycle still depends on that presentation, so in order to isolate the part determined by the cohomology class itself we pass to the homological evaluation map. Throughout the section we work with one fixed finite cover and the comparison maps coming from the identification of \v{C}ech cochains with simplicial cochains on its finite nerve. The resulting hidden subgroup and visible quotient are finite-nerve-class invariants only relative to the homology-equivalent changes of presentation singled out below.
    5: 
    6: We also assume that the chosen cover $\mathcal U$ trivializes the band, so that the coefficients may be viewed as a constant finite abelian group $A$ on the nerve. The case of nontrivial monodromy, which would require local coefficients, is not considered here.
    7: 
    8: \subsection{Finite nerve presentations}
    9: 
   10: \begin{definition}[Finite nerve presentation of the obstruction class]\label{def:finite-nerve-presentation}
   11: Fix an admitted reference $r:\mathsf{Ref}_s$ at a state $p$, and assume conditions \textup{(i)}--\textup{(iv)} of \Cref{def:finite-obstruction-presentation}, with the ordered \v{C}ech convention of \Cref{def:ordered-cech-convention}. A \emph{finite nerve presentation} of the branch obstruction class consists of:
   12: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
   13: \item a finite covering family
   14: \[
   15: \mathcal U=\{a_i\to a\}_{i\in I},
   16: \qquad
   17: a=[r]_p,
   18: \]
   19: whose \v{C}ech nerve $N(\mathcal U)$ is finite;
   20: \item a trivialization of the band on $\mathcal U$, so that the band may be viewed as a constant coefficient finite abelian group $A$ on $N(\mathcal U)$;
   21: \item the induced finite-nerve class
   22: \[
   23: \omega\in H^2(N(\mathcal U),A),
   24: \]
   25: obtained by identifying \v{C}ech cochains on $\mathcal U$ with simplicial cochains on the finite nerve, transporting the branch obstruction cocycle through that identification, and writing
   26: \[
   27: \omega:=\omega_v
   28: \]
   29: for the chosen visible branch. When the class is also viewed as a derived
   30: $H^2$-class, the presentation includes either the
   31: \v{C}ech--derived comparison package of
   32: \Cref{def:cech-derived-comparison-package} or the finite good-cover Leray
   33: comparison of \Cref{lem:generated-subsite-model-interface}. Without one of
   34: these comparison inputs, \(\omega\) is only the selected finite-nerve
   35: \v{C}ech/simplicial class.
   36: \end{enumerate}
   37: Write
   38: \[
   39: \eta_A:H^2(N(\mathcal U),A)\longrightarrow \mathrm{Hom}(H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),A)
   40: \]
   41: for the map in the universal coefficient exact sequence
   42: \[
   43: 0\to \operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),A)\to H^2(N(\mathcal U),A)\xrightarrow{\eta_A}\mathrm{Hom}(H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),A)\to 0
   44: \]
   45: \cite[Sec.~3.1]{Hatcher2002}, and define the \emph{homological evaluation map}
   46: \[
   47: \mathrm{ev}_{\omega}:=\eta_A(\omega).
   48: \]
   49: \end{definition}
   50: 
   51: \begin{definition}[Finite nerve comparison groupoid]\label{def:finite-nerve-comparison-relation}
   52: For finite nerve presentations of a fixed branch obstruction class, an
   53: \emph{elementary comparison} is one of the three operations listed in
   54: \Cref{prop:invariance-visible-quotients}: homology-equivalent refinement,
   55: cohomologous cocycle replacement, or re-trivialization of the constant band.
   56: The \emph{finite nerve comparison groupoid} has these presentations as
   57: objects and finite zigzags of elementary comparisons and formal inverses as
   58: morphisms, with composition given by concatenation of zigzags. We say that two
   59: presentations are comparison-equivalent when they lie in the same connected
   60: component of this groupoid. Thus the comparison data carried by a finite
   61: zigzag are part of the pseudofunctorial assignment; no path independence
   62: between distinct zigzags is asserted unless an additional coherence
   63: hypothesis is imposed.
   64: \end{definition}
   65: 
   66: \begin{proposition}[Pseudofunctorial assignment of $K_\omega$ and $A_{\mathrm{vis}}^\omega$]\label{prop:invariance-visible-quotients}
   67: Let $(\mathcal U,A,\omega)$ and $(\mathcal U',A',\omega')$ be finite nerve presentations of the same branch obstruction class. Suppose first that the two presentations differ by one of the following elementary comparisons:
   68: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
   69: \item refinement of the cover by a simplicial map
   70: \[
   71: f:N(\mathcal U')\to N(\mathcal U)
   72: \]
   73: from the refined nerve to the original nerve, such that
   74: \[
   75: \omega'=f^*\omega
   76: \]
   77: and
   78: \[
   79: f_*:H_2(N(\mathcal U'),\mathbb Z)\xrightarrow{\sim} H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)
   80: \]
   81: is an isomorphism, with the coefficient group fixed;
   82: \item replacement of the \v{C}ech cocycle by a cohomologous cocycle in $H^2(N(\mathcal U),A)$;
   83: \item re-trivialization of the band by an isomorphism of coefficient groups
   84: \[
   85: \theta:A\xrightarrow{\sim} A',
   86: \]
   87: so that
   88: \[
   89: \omega'=\theta_*\omega.
   90: \]
   91: \end{enumerate}
   92: Then there exists a specified coefficient comparison isomorphism $\phi:A\to A'$---the identity in \textup{(i)} and \textup{(ii)}, and $\theta$ in \textup{(iii)}---such that
   93: \[
   94: \phi\bigl(K_{\omega}\bigr)=K_{\omega'}
   95: \qquad\text{and}\qquad
   96: \bar\phi:A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}=A/K_\omega\xrightarrow{\sim}A'/K_{\omega'}=A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega'}
   97: \]
   98: is the induced quotient isomorphism. In particular, both $K_{\omega}$ and $A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}$ are transported by the three elementary comparison operations listed above, with the refinement direction fixed by the displayed pullback map.
   99: 
  100: More generally, if the two presentations are joined by a morphism $\Gamma$ in
  101: the finite nerve comparison groupoid of
  102: \Cref{def:finite-nerve-comparison-relation}, then the chosen finite comparison
  103: zigzag $\Gamma$ determines, by composition of the elementary coefficient
  104: comparisons and their inverses, an isomorphism
  105: \[
  106: \phi_\Gamma:A\xrightarrow{\sim} A'
  107: \]
  108: such that
  109: \[
  110: \phi_\Gamma(K_\omega)=K_{\omega'}
  111: \qquad\text{and}\qquad
  112: A/K_\omega\xrightarrow{\sim}A'/K_{\omega'}
  113: \]
  114: is the corresponding composite quotient isomorphism. Hence
  115: $(\mathcal U,A,\omega)\mapsto (K_\omega\le A)$ and
  116: $(\mathcal U,A,\omega)\mapsto A_{\mathrm{vis}}^\omega$ define a pseudofunctorial assignment over the
  117: finite nerve comparison groupoid. They are presentation-object canonical:
  118: once a finite nerve, coefficient trivialization, and comparison package have
  119: been fixed, the subgroup and quotient are determined by that object. Along a
  120: chosen comparison zigzag they are transported functorially by the displayed
  121: composite isomorphism. The statement does not assert path independence, and
  122: it does not identify the isomorphisms attached to two different zigzags with
  123: the same endpoints.
  124: \end{proposition}
  125: 
  126: \begin{remark}[Typical refinements]\label{rem:refinement-iso}
  127: The refinements used later are simplicial homotopy equivalences on the chosen nerves---for example, barycentric subdivisions and the standard refinements attached to good covers---and therefore satisfy the hypothesis of case \textup{(i)} of \Cref{prop:invariance-visible-quotients}.
  128: \end{remark}
  129: 
  130: \begin{remark}[Necessity of homology equivalences and chosen paths]\label{rem:homology-equality}
  131: The equalities in \Cref{prop:invariance-visible-quotients} rely on the assumption that the refinement map induces an isomorphism on $H_2$. Without that hypothesis, for a refinement \(f:N(\mathcal U')\to N(\mathcal U)\) with \(\omega'=f^*\omega\), naturality gives
  132: \[
  133: K_{\omega'}=\operatorname{Im}(\mathrm{ev}_{\omega}\circ f_*)\subseteq K_{\omega}.
  134: \]
  135: Hence one obtains \(K_{\omega'}\subseteq K_{\omega}\), and therefore a comparison map
  136: \[
  137: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega'}=A/K_{\omega'}\twoheadrightarrow
  138: A/K_{\omega}=A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega},
  139: \]
  140: but not equality. The term ``finite-nerve-class visible quotient'' is therefore reserved for refinements and presentations that are homology-equivalent in this precise sense.
  141: 
  142: For presentations connected by several finite zigzags, the comparison
  143: isomorphism is part of the finite nerve comparison groupoid. A different
  144: zigzag may compose the re-trivialization automorphisms of the coefficient band
  145: in a different way, and the paper does not quotient by that possible holonomy.
  146: Thus the assignment is pseudofunctorial over the finite nerve comparison groupoid rather than a path-independent quotient
  147: on the set of comparison-equivalence classes.
  148: \end{remark}
  149: 
  150: \begin{proof}
  151: Case \textup{(ii)} is immediate because cohomologous cocycles define the same element of $H^2(N(\mathcal U),A)$ and hence have the same image under $\eta_A$. Here $\phi=\mathrm{id}_A$, so the quotient map is the identity on $A/K_\omega$.
  152: 
  153: In case \textup{(i)}, the assumption on $f$ gives an isomorphism on $H_2$. Since $\omega'=f^*(\omega)$ and the universal coefficient map is natural, one has
  154: \[
  155: \mathrm{ev}_{\omega'}=\eta_A\bigl(f^*(\omega)\bigr)=\eta_A(\omega)\circ f_*,
  156: \]
  157: where $f_*:H_2(N(\mathcal U'),\mathbb Z)\to H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)$ is induced on homology and is an isomorphism by hypothesis. Hence $\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{ev}_{\omega'})=\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{ev}_\omega)$, so $K_{\omega'}=K_{\omega}$ and $A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega'}\cong A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}$.
  158: The comparison is canonical because the refinement operation supplies the direction of $f$ and hence the specified isomorphism $f_*$; replacing $f_*$ by its inverse would compare the evaluation maps in the wrong direction. Thus the subgroup equality occurs inside the same coefficient group $A$, and the quotient isomorphism is induced by $\phi=\mathrm{id}_A$.
  159: 
  160: In case \textup{(iii)}, the coefficient isomorphism $\theta$ identifies the two cohomology groups, and $\omega'=\theta_*(\omega)$. Naturality gives the coefficient-isomorphism square
  161: \[
  162: \begin{array}{ccc}
  163: H^2(N(\mathcal U),A) & \xrightarrow{\eta_A} &
  164: \mathrm{Hom}(H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),A)\\
  165: \theta_*\downarrow\phantom{\theta_*} && \phantom{\theta\circ(-)}\downarrow\theta\circ(-)\\
  166: H^2(N(\mathcal U),A') & \xrightarrow{\eta_{A'}} &
  167: \mathrm{Hom}(H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),A'),
  168: \end{array}
  169: \]
  170: and hence
  171: \[
  172: \mathrm{ev}_{\omega'}=\eta_{A'}(\omega')=\eta_{A'}\bigl(\theta_*(\omega)\bigr)=\theta\circ \eta_A(\omega)=\theta\circ \mathrm{ev}_\omega.
  173: \]
  174: Therefore $K_{\omega'}=\theta(K_{\omega})$, and $\theta$ induces an isomorphism between $A_{\mathrm{vis}}^\omega$ and $A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega'}$.
  175: 
  176: In all three cases the induced quotient map is well defined because the chosen coefficient comparison $\phi$ sends $K_\omega$ onto $K_{\omega'}$. Its inverse is induced by the inverse coefficient comparison: the identity in cases \textup{(i)} and \textup{(ii)}, and $\theta^{-1}$ in case \textup{(iii)}. This proves functoriality of the specified elementary comparison and invariance of the quotient for that elementary morphism.
  177: 
  178: For a finite zigzag in the comparison groupoid, apply the elementary
  179: case at each step. If a step is traversed in the opposite direction, use the
  180: inverse coefficient and quotient isomorphisms just constructed. Composition of
  181: the finitely many subgroup identifications gives
  182: \[
  183: \phi_\Gamma(K_\omega)=K_{\omega'},
  184: \]
  185: and the universal property of quotients gives the displayed quotient
  186: isomorphism. Concatenating two zigzags concatenates the corresponding composite
  187: coefficient maps, so these assignments respect identity zigzags and composition
  188: of zigzags. Thus finite composites of the three comparison operations preserve
  189: the finite-nerve-class hidden subgroup and visible quotient in the stated
  190: groupoid-indexed sense.
  191: \end{proof}
  192: 
  193: \begin{proposition}[Finite nerve comparison isomorphism]\label{prop:finite-nerve-comparison}
  194: Work in one finite nerve presentation
  195: $(\mathcal U,A,\omega)$ of \Cref{def:finite-nerve-presentation}; in
  196: particular, the band has been trivialized on $\mathcal U$ and is being
  197: viewed as the constant finite abelian group $A$ on the selected finite
  198: nerve. Use the total order on the finite index set $I$ already fixed in
  199: \Cref{def:ordered-cech-convention} for
  200: \[
  201: \mathcal U=\{a_i\to a\}_{i\in I}.
  202: \]
  203: Use the ordered \v{C}ech convention in which $\check C^n(\mathcal U,A)$
  204: is indexed by tuples
  205: \[
  206: i_0<\cdots<i_n
  207: \]
  208: with nonempty overlap, and use the corresponding ordered oriented
  209: simplices of $N(\mathcal U)$ for simplicial cochains. For each $n\ge 0$,
  210: each nonempty ordered $(n{+}1)$-fold overlap $a_{i_0\cdots i_n}$
  211: determines the oriented $n$-simplex $(i_0,\dots,i_n)$ of the selected
  212: nerve, and the assignment
  213: \[
  214: \Theta_{\mathcal U}^n:\check C^n(\mathcal U,A)\to C^n(N(\mathcal U),A),\qquad
  215: (\alpha_{i_0\cdots i_n})\longmapsto\bigl((i_0,\dots,i_n)\mapsto \alpha_{i_0\cdots i_n}\bigr)
  216: \]
  217: is an isomorphism of cochain groups. Under this correspondence,
  218: \[
  219: \delta\leftrightarrow d,\qquad
  220: B^n\leftrightarrow B^n,\qquad
  221: Z^n\leftrightarrow Z^n.
  222: \]
  223: Hence the \v{C}ech cohomology of this selected ordered finite nerve
  224: presentation with constant coefficients $A$ is identified with the
  225: simplicial cohomology of $N(\mathcal U)$ with coefficients in $A$.
  226: \end{proposition}
  227: \begin{proof}
  228: The hypotheses are part of the finite nerve presentation: the cover is
  229: finite, the coefficient sheaf has been trivialized to the constant group
  230: $A$ on this cover, and the comparison is being made on the chosen finite
  231: nerve rather than on an arbitrary refinement system. With the fixed order
  232: on $I$, the $n$-cochains on $\mathcal U$ are families of coefficients
  233: indexed by nonempty ordered $(n{+}1)$-fold overlaps
  234: $a_{i_0\cdots i_n}$ with $i_0<\cdots<i_n$, while the simplicial
  235: $n$-cochains on $N(\mathcal U)$ are coefficient functions on the
  236: corresponding ordered oriented $n$-simplices $(i_0,\dots,i_n)$. By
  237: definition of the selected nerve, these two index sets agree, so
  238: $\Theta_{\mathcal U}^n$ is a bijection.
  239: 
  240: The \v{C}ech coboundary of $(\alpha_{i_0\cdots i_n})$ is the alternating
  241: sum obtained by omitting the $k$th ordered index, and the simplicial
  242: coboundary of $\Theta_{\mathcal U}^n(\alpha)$ uses the same ordered face
  243: map with the same sign $(-1)^k$. Since the coefficients are constant
  244: under the chosen trivialization, the restriction maps on coefficients are
  245: the identity maps of $A$. Because the face maps of $N(\mathcal U)$ are
  246: induced by omitting one ordered index, the two alternating sums coincide
  247: term by term. Hence
  248: \[
  249: \Theta_{\mathcal U}^{n+1}(\delta\alpha)=d(\Theta_{\mathcal U}^n(\alpha)).
  250: \]
  251: Therefore $\Theta_{\mathcal U}^\bullet$ is an isomorphism of cochain
  252: complexes, and it induces the stated identifications on cocycles,
  253: coboundaries, and cohomology. This is only the finite ordered
  254: constant-coefficient comparison used in \Cref{def:finite-nerve-presentation};
  255: promotion of a filtered \v{C}ech system to derived cohomology remains the
  256: separate \textup{(H4)} assumption of \Cref{rem:cech-vs-derived}.
  257: \end{proof}
  258: 
  259: \begin{proposition}[Strict versus finite-nerve-class hidden subgroups]\label{prop:strict-vs-intrinsic-hidden-subgroups}
  260: In the setting of \Cref{def:finite-nerve-presentation}, let $\alpha\in Z^2(N(\mathcal U),A)$ be a simplicial cocycle representing $\omega$, and let
  261: \[
  262: \widetilde\alpha:C_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)\to A
  263: \]
  264: be the chain-level evaluation map obtained by linear extension from the oriented $2$-simplices. Then
  265: \[
  266: H_\alpha=\operatorname{Im}(\widetilde\alpha)
  267: \]
  268: and
  269: \[
  270: K_{\omega}:=\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{ev}_{\omega}).
  271: \]
  272: Thus $H_\alpha$ records presentation-level chain visibility: it is generated by the values of the chosen cocycle on all $2$-chains. By contrast, $K_{\omega}$ records cycle-level class visibility: it is the image that survives after passage to $2$-homology. In particular,
  273: \[
  274: K_{\omega}\subseteq H_\alpha.
  275: \]
  276: \end{proposition}
  277: \begin{proof}
  278: The image of $\widetilde\alpha$ is the subgroup generated by the values of $\alpha$ on the oriented $2$-simplices, equivalently by the values of the original \v{C}ech cocycle on the nonempty triple overlaps of $\mathcal U$. This is exactly the strict subgroup attached to the chosen presentation. The second equality is the definition of $K_\omega$. Since $H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)$ is represented by $2$-cycles inside $C_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)$, every homological value counted by $\mathrm{ev}_\omega$ is, a fortiori, a chain-level value of $\widetilde\alpha$, so $K_\omega\subseteq H_\alpha$.
  279: \end{proof}
  280: 
    1: \subsection{Finite-nerve-class visibility and admissible characters}
    2: 
    3: \begin{definition}[Finite-nerve-class visible quotient]\label{def:intrinsic-visible-quotient}
    4: In the setting of \Cref{def:finite-nerve-presentation}, define the \emph{finite-nerve-class hidden subgroup}
    5: \[
    6: K_{\omega}:=\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{ev}_{\omega})
    7: \]
    8: and the associated \emph{finite-nerve-class visible quotient}
    9: \[
   10: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}:=\operatorname{coker}(\mathrm{ev}_{\omega})=A/K_{\omega}.
   11: \]
   12: These terms are relative to the finite nerve comparison groupoid of
   13: \Cref{def:finite-nerve-comparison-relation}; no presentation-free quotient is
   14: asserted outside that groupoid.
   15: \end{definition}
   16: 
   17: \begin{definition}[Class-admissible characters]\label{def:class-admissible-character}
   18: In the setting of \Cref{def:finite-nerve-presentation}, let
   19: \[
   20: X_{\omega}:=\{\chi\in\widehat A\mid \chi_*(\omega)=0\},
   21: \qquad
   22: \widehat A:=\mathrm{Hom}(A,\mathbb T).
   23: \]
   24: Elements of $X_{\omega}$ are called \emph{class-admissible} for $\omega$.
   25: \end{definition}
   26: 
   27: \begin{theorem}[Character-theoretic detection of the finite-nerve-class visible quotient]\label{thm:intrinsic-visible-quotient}
   28: In the setting of \Cref{def:class-admissible-character},
   29: \[
   30: X_{\omega}
   31: =
   32: \{\chi\in\widehat A\mid \chi\circ \mathrm{ev}_{\omega}=0\}
   33: =
   34: \mathrm{Ann}(K_{\omega}).
   35: \]
   36: Consequently,
   37: \[
   38: K_{\omega}
   39: =
   40: \bigcap_{\chi\in X_{\omega}}\ker\chi,
   41: \qquad
   42: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}\cong A\Big/\!\bigcap_{\chi\in X_{\omega}}\ker\chi.
   43: \]
   44: \end{theorem}
   45: 
   46: \begin{proof}
   47: For every character $\chi:A\to\mathbb T$, naturality of the universal coefficient sequence gives a commutative square
   48: \[
   49: \begin{CD}
   50: H^2(N(\mathcal U),A) @>\eta_A>> \mathrm{Hom}(H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),A) \\
   51: @V\chi_*VV @VV(\chi\circ -)V \\
   52: H^2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb T) @>\eta_{\mathbb T}>> \mathrm{Hom}(H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),\mathbb T).
   53: \end{CD}
   54: \]
   55: Since $\mathbb T$ is divisible and hence injective in $\mathbf{Ab}$, one has
   56: \[
   57: \operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),\mathbb T)=0
   58: \]
   59: \cite[Sec.~2.3]{Weibel1994}. Thus $\eta_{\mathbb T}$ is an isomorphism by \Cref{def:finite-nerve-presentation}. Therefore
   60: \[
   61: \chi_*(\omega)=0
   62: \Longleftrightarrow
   63: \eta_{\mathbb T}(\chi_*(\omega))=0
   64: \Longleftrightarrow
   65: \chi\circ \eta_A(\omega)=0
   66: \Longleftrightarrow
   67: \chi\circ \mathrm{ev}_{\omega}=0.
   68: \]
   69: This proves the first displayed identity, and the second is simply the definition of the annihilator of a subgroup of $A$.
   70: 
   71: Let $H:=K_{\omega}=\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{ev}_{\omega})\le A$. By the first part of the proof, $X_{\omega}=\mathrm{Ann}(H)$. Finite abelian duality implies
   72: \[
   73: \bigcap_{\chi\in \mathrm{Ann}(H)}\ker\chi=H
   74: \]
   75: \cite[Part~I, Chaps.~2, 12]{Terras1999}. Hence
   76: \[
   77: K_{\omega}
   78: =
   79: \bigcap_{\chi\in X_{\omega}}\ker\chi.
   80: \]
   81: The final isomorphism follows immediately:
   82: \[
   83: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}
   84: =
   85: A/K_{\omega}
   86: \cong
   87: A\Big/\!\bigcap_{\chi\in X_{\omega}}\ker\chi.
   88: \qedhere
   89: \]
   90: \end{proof}
   91: 
   92: \begin{corollary}[Character detection of the finite-nerve-class visible quotient]\label{cor:intrinsic-character-detection}
   93: The quotient map
   94: \[
   95: \pi_{\omega}:A\twoheadrightarrow A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}
   96: \]
   97: induces a natural isomorphism
   98: \[
   99: \widehat{A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}}\cong X_{\omega}.
  100: \]
  101: For elements $x,y\in A$, one has
  102: \[
  103: \pi_{\omega}(x)=\pi_{\omega}(y)
  104: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  105: \chi(x)=\chi(y)\ \text{for every class-admissible }\chi.
  106: \]
  107: \end{corollary}
  108: 
  109: \begin{proof}
  110: By \Cref{thm:intrinsic-visible-quotient},
  111: \[
  112: X_{\omega}=\mathrm{Ann}(K_{\omega})=K_{\omega}^{\perp}.
  113: \]
  114: Characters of $A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}=A/K_{\omega}$ are exactly the characters of $A$ that kill $K_{\omega}$, so precomposition with $\pi_{\omega}$ identifies $\widehat{A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}}$ with $X_{\omega}$. The separation statement follows from finite abelian duality applied to the finite group $A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}$ \cite[Part~I, Chap.~2]{Terras1999}.
  115: \end{proof}
  116: 
  117: \begin{theorem}[Initiality among pure-extension quotients]\label{thm:intrinsic-pure-ext-initiality}
  118: In the finite nerve presentation of \Cref{def:finite-nerve-presentation}, let
  119: \[
  120: \pi_\omega:A\twoheadrightarrow A_{\mathrm{vis}}^\omega=A/K_\omega
  121: \]
  122: be the finite-nerve-class visible quotient. For every surjective homomorphism $q:A\twoheadrightarrow B$, the following are equivalent:
  123: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  124: \item $q$ factors uniquely through $\pi_\omega$;
  125: \item $q$ kills $\operatorname{Im}(\mathrm{ev}_\omega)=K_\omega$;
  126: \item $\eta_B(q_*\omega)=0$;
  127: \item $q_*(\omega)$ lies in the pure $\operatorname{Ext}$ subgroup
  128: \[
  129: \operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),B)
  130: \subseteq H^2(N(\mathcal U),B)
  131: \]
  132: of the universal coefficient sequence.
  133: \end{enumerate}
  134: Thus $A_{\mathrm{vis}}^\omega$ is initial among coefficient quotients for which the pushed-forward obstruction has no $H_2$-visible part. This is the pullback-site, finite-nerve form of the companion initiality theorem \cite[Theorem~6.13]{MaZhang2026ConditionalGluingFailure}.
  135: \end{theorem}
  136: 
  137: \begin{proof}
  138: The equivalence of \textup{(i)} and \textup{(ii)} is the universal property of the quotient
  139: \[
  140: \pi_\omega:A\twoheadrightarrow A/K_\omega.
  141: \]
  142: Indeed, a homomorphism $q:A\to B$ factors uniquely through $\pi_\omega$ if and only if $K_\omega\subseteq\ker q$, which is exactly the assertion that $q$ kills $\operatorname{Im}(\mathrm{ev}_\omega)$.
  143: 
  144: For \textup{(ii)} and \textup{(iii)}, use naturality of the universal coefficient map with respect to the coefficient homomorphism $q$. The naturality square gives
  145: \[
  146: \eta_B(q_*\omega)=q\circ \eta_A(\omega)=q\circ \mathrm{ev}_\omega.
  147: \]
  148: Thus $\eta_B(q_*\omega)=0$ if and only if $q$ vanishes on the image of $\mathrm{ev}_\omega$, namely on $K_\omega$.
  149: 
  150: Finally, the universal coefficient exact sequence for the finite nerve is
  151: \[
  152: 0\to \operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),B)
  153: \to H^2(N(\mathcal U),B)
  154: \xrightarrow{\eta_B}
  155: \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),B)
  156: \to 0.
  157: \]
  158: Therefore $\eta_B(q_*\omega)=0$ if and only if $q_*(\omega)$ lies in the kernel of $\eta_B$, which is precisely the displayed pure $\operatorname{Ext}$ subgroup. This proves all equivalences and the initiality statement.
  159: \end{proof}
  160: 
  161: \begin{corollary}[Class-trivializing initiality when $H_1$ vanishes]\label{cor:h1-vanishing-class-trivializing}
  162: Assume
  163: \[
  164: H_1(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)=0.
  165: \]
  166: Then, for every surjective homomorphism $q:A\twoheadrightarrow B$, the following are equivalent:
  167: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  168: \item $q_*(\omega)=0$ in $H^2(N(\mathcal U),B)$;
  169: \item $q$ factors uniquely through
  170: \[
  171: \pi_{\omega}:A\twoheadrightarrow A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}.
  172: \]
  173: \end{enumerate}
  174: Hence, when $H_1(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)=0$, the finite-nerve-class visible quotient is initial among coefficient quotients that annihilate the obstruction class itself.
  175: \end{corollary}
  176: 
  177: \begin{proof}
  178: If $H_1(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)=0$, then
  179: \[
  180: \operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),B)=0
  181: \]
  182: for every abelian group $B$. Thus, in the criterion of \Cref{thm:intrinsic-pure-ext-initiality}, the pure-extension condition is equivalent to $q_*(\omega)=0$, and the conclusion follows.
  183: \end{proof}
  184: 
  185: \begin{remark}[Presentation-object visibility without a canonical splitting]\label{rem:no-canonical-splitting}
  186: For a fixed finite nerve presentation, the quotient
  187: $A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}$ and the subgroup
  188: $K_{\omega}=\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{ev}_{\omega})$ are presentation-object
  189: canonical. Across comparison-equivalent presentations they are transported
  190: functorially only along chosen morphisms in the finite nerve comparison
  191: groupoid, as in \Cref{prop:invariance-visible-quotients}; different zigzags
  192: with the same endpoints may carry different coefficient automorphisms. By
  193: contrast, the universal coefficient sequence need not split canonically. Thus
  194: the theory isolates a finite-nerve-class visible quotient and a blind subgroup
  195: at each presentation, with comparison-zigzag functoriality, without requiring
  196: any noncanonical decomposition of $\omega$ into visible and invisible
  197: summands.
  198: \end{remark}
  199: 
  200: \begin{corollary}[Comparison with strict visible quotients]\label{cor:strict-to-intrinsic-visible}
  201: Let $\alpha$ be any finite obstruction presentation of the class $\omega$. Then the finite-nerve-class visible quotient
  202: \[
  203: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}
  204: \]
  205: admits a canonical surjective homomorphism onto the strict visible quotient
  206: \[
  207: A/H_\alpha.
  208: \]
  209: Equivalently, every strict visible quotient is a quotient of the finite-nerve-class visible quotient.
  210: \end{corollary}
  211: 
  212: \begin{proof}
  213: Let $q:A\twoheadrightarrow A/H_\alpha$ be the canonical quotient. By \Cref{thm:initial-visible-quotient}, the quotient kills every cocycle value $\alpha_{ijk}$ pointwise, so the pushed-forward cocycle representative is zero. Hence $q_*(\omega)=0$, and in particular it lies in the $\operatorname{Ext}$-subgroup of $H^2(N(\mathcal U),A/H_\alpha)$. Apply \Cref{thm:intrinsic-pure-ext-initiality}.
  214: \end{proof}
  215: 
  216: \begin{definition}[Chain-level evaluation of a presentation]\label{def:chain-level-evaluation}
  217: Let $\alpha\in Z^2(N(\mathcal U),A)$ be a finite obstruction presentation of the class $\omega$. Write
  218: \[
  219: C_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)
  220: \]
  221: for the simplicial group of integral $2$-chains on the finite nerve. The \emph{chain-level evaluation map} of $\alpha$ is the homomorphism
  222: \[
  223: \widetilde\alpha:C_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)\to A
  224: \]
  225: defined by linear extension from the oriented $2$-simplices:
  226: \[
  227: \widetilde\alpha\!\left(\sum_{\sigma} n_{\sigma}\sigma\right)
  228: :=
  229: \sum_{\sigma} n_{\sigma}\alpha(\sigma).
  230: \]
  231: \end{definition}
  232: 
  233: \begin{proposition}[Strict visibility versus homological visibility]\label{prop:chain-vs-homological-visibility}
  234: In the setting of \Cref{def:chain-level-evaluation}, the following hold.
  235: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  236: \item $\widetilde\alpha$ vanishes on $2$-boundaries and therefore induces a homomorphism
  237: \[
  238: \overline\alpha:H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)\to A.
  239: \]
  240: \item The induced homomorphism $\overline\alpha$ is exactly the homological evaluation map $\mathrm{ev}_{\omega}$.
  241: \item The strict obstruction subgroup and the finite-nerve-class hidden subgroup satisfy
  242: \[
  243: H_\alpha=\mathrm{Im}(\widetilde\alpha),
  244: \qquad
  245: K_{\omega}=\mathrm{Im}(\overline\alpha)=\widetilde\alpha(Z_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)).
  246: \]
  247: \end{enumerate}
  248: Thus the gap between strict visibility and intrinsic visibility is exactly the gap between the image of the cocycle on all $2$-chains and its image on $2$-cycles.
  249: \end{proposition}
  250: 
  251: \begin{proof}
  252: If $c\in C_3(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)$, then
  253: \[
  254: \widetilde\alpha(\partial c)=(\delta\alpha)(c)=0
  255: \]
  256: because $\alpha$ is a cocycle. Hence $\widetilde\alpha$ vanishes on $B_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)$ and induces a homomorphism
  257: \[
  258: \overline\alpha:H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)=Z_2/B_2\to A.
  259: \]
  260: This proves \textup{(i)}.
  261: 
  262: For any $2$-cycle $z=\sum_{\sigma} n_{\sigma}\sigma\in Z_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)$, set
  263: \[
  264: \alpha^{\sharp}(z):=\sum_{\sigma} n_{\sigma}\alpha(\sigma)=\widetilde\alpha(z).
  265: \]
  266: If $z'$ is another $2$-cycle homologous to $z$, then $z-z'=\partial c$ for some $c\in C_3(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)$. By \textup{(i)},
  267: \[
  268: \alpha^{\sharp}(z)-\alpha^{\sharp}(z')
  269: =
  270: \widetilde\alpha(z-z')
  271: =
  272: \widetilde\alpha(\partial c)
  273: =
  274: 0.
  275: \]
  276: Therefore $\alpha^{\sharp}$ depends only on the homology class of $z$ and defines a homomorphism
  277: \[
  278: H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)\to A,\qquad
  279: [z]\longmapsto \alpha^{\sharp}(z).
  280: \]
  281: By the definition of the universal coefficient map $\eta_A$, this homomorphism is exactly the image of the cohomology class $\omega=[\alpha]$ under
  282: \[
  283: \eta_A:H^2(N(\mathcal U),A)\to \operatorname{Hom}(H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),A).
  284: \]
  285: Thus
  286: \[
  287: \overline\alpha([z])=\alpha^{\sharp}(z)=\eta_A(\omega)([z])=\mathrm{ev}_{\omega}([z])
  288: \]
  289: for every $[z]\in H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)$, proving \textup{(ii)}.
  290: 
  291: For \textup{(iii)}, the image of $\widetilde\alpha$ is exactly the subgroup generated by the simplex values $\alpha_{ijk}$, hence it is $H_\alpha$ by \Cref{def:finite-obstruction-presentation}. The equality
  292: \[
  293: \mathrm{Im}(\overline\alpha)=\widetilde\alpha(Z_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z))
  294: \]
  295: is immediate from \textup{(i)}, and \Cref{def:intrinsic-visible-quotient} identifies this image with $K_{\omega}$.
  296: \end{proof}
  297: 
  298: \begin{corollary}[Criterion for coincidence of strict and intrinsic visibility]\label{cor:strict-intrinsic-coincidence-criterion}
  299: Let $\alpha$ be a finite obstruction presentation of $\omega$. The following are equivalent:
  300: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  301: \item $A/H_\alpha\cong A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}$;
  302: \item $H_\alpha=K_{\omega}$;
  303: \item $\widetilde\alpha(C_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z))=\widetilde\alpha(Z_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z))$.
  304: \end{enumerate}
  305: Equivalently, strict visibility agrees with intrinsic visibility exactly when every presentation-level visible value already arises from the evaluation of the cocycle on a genuine $2$-cycle.
  306: \end{corollary}
  307: 
  308: \begin{proof}
  309: The equivalence of \textup{(i)} and \textup{(ii)} is immediate from the quotient descriptions
  310: \[
  311: A/H_\alpha
  312: \qquad\text{and}\qquad
  313: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}=A/K_{\omega}.
  314: \]
  315: The equivalence of \textup{(ii)} and \textup{(iii)} is exactly \Cref{prop:chain-vs-homological-visibility}(iii).
  316: \end{proof}
  317: 
  318: \begin{theorem}[Character-blind pure-extension obstructions]\label{thm:character-blind-obstructions}
  319: In the setting of \Cref{def:finite-nerve-presentation}, with a chosen admitted
  320: reference, visible branch, realization prestack, trivialized constant finite
  321: abelian band $A$, and the \textup{(H4)} comparison datum needed to regard
  322: $\omega$ as a derived branch class, the following are equivalent:
  323: \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),nosep]
  324: \item $K_{\omega}=0$;
  325: \item $A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}=A$;
  326: \item $\mathrm{ev}_{\omega}=0$;
  327: \item $\omega$ lies in the subgroup
  328: \[
  329: \operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),A)\subseteq H^2(N(\mathcal U),A).
  330: \]
  331: \end{enumerate}
  332: If these conditions hold and $\omega\neq 0$, then the chosen branch gerbe $\mathfrak G_v$ is non-neutral, while every character of $A$ is class-admissible for that branch. If, in addition, the realization prestack is globally conservative at the terminal fibre, \(F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}\) is slice-separated, and $|\Vis_{p,s}(r)|=1$, then
  333: \[
  334: M,p\Vdash \Null^{\mathrm{glue}}_s(r).
  335: \]
  336: \end{theorem}
  337: 
  338: \begin{proof}
  339: By \Cref{def:intrinsic-visible-quotient},
  340: \[
  341: K_{\omega}=\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{ev}_{\omega}),
  342: \]
  343: so \textup{(i)} and \textup{(iii)} are equivalent. Since $A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}=A/K_{\omega}$, conditions \textup{(i)} and \textup{(ii)} are equivalent. By the universal coefficient sequence, \textup{(iii)} holds exactly when $\omega\in\ker(\eta_A)$, and this kernel is the stated $\operatorname{Ext}$-subgroup. Hence all four conditions are equivalent.
  344: 
  345: If they hold and $\omega\neq 0$, then \Cref{prop:derived-branch-classification} shows that the branch gerbe $\mathfrak G_v$ is non-neutral.
  346: On the other hand, $\mathrm{ev}_{\omega}=0$ implies
  347: \[
  348: \chi\circ \mathrm{ev}_{\omega}=0
  349: \]
  350: for every $\chi\in\widehat A$, so \Cref{thm:intrinsic-visible-quotient} shows that every character is class-admissible.
  351: 
  352: Assume now that \(F_{p,s}|_{\mathcal C_a}\) is slice-separated and that $|\Vis_{p,s}(r)|=1$. Since the chosen branch $v$ exists, \Cref{prop:compsecs-vs-visible-branches} gives
  353: \[
  354: M,p\Vdash \CompSec_s(r).
  355: \]
  356: Suppose towards a contradiction that
  357: \[
  358: M,p\Vdash \Sec_s(r).
  359: \]
  360: By \Cref{def:realization-prestack}(i), this yields an object of the terminal fibre $(\mathfrak P_r)_a$, and its image under the stackification morphism
  361: \[
  362: \iota_r:\mathfrak P_r\to \mathfrak L_r
  363: \]
  364: defines an object of $\mathfrak L_r(a)$. Because there is only one visible branch, every object of $\mathfrak L_r(a)$ lies in the unique component gerbe $\mathfrak G_v(a)$. Hence
  365: \[
  366: \mathfrak G_v(a)\neq\varnothing.
  367: \]
  368: By \Cref{lem:terminal-fibre-neutrality}, this implies that $\mathfrak G_v$ is neutral, contradicting the non-neutrality already proved. Therefore
  369: \[
  370: M,p\nVdash \Sec_s(r).
  371: \]
  372: Combining this with $M,p\Vdash \CompSec_s(r)$ and admittedness of $r$, we obtain
  373: \[
  374: M,p\Vdash \Null^{\mathrm{glue}}_s(r).
  375: \]
  376: \end{proof}
  377: 
  378: \begin{corollary}[Automatic blindness when $H_2$ vanishes]\label{cor:h2-vanishing-blindness}
  379: Assume
  380: \[
  381: H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)=0.
  382: \]
  383: Then, for every obstruction class $\omega\in H^2(N(\mathcal U),A)$,
  384: \[
  385: \mathrm{ev}_{\omega}=0,
  386: \qquad
  387: K_{\omega}=0,
  388: \qquad
  389: A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}=A.
  390: \]
  391: Every nonzero branch obstruction class on such a nerve is therefore character-blind.
  392: \end{corollary}
  393: 
  394: \begin{proof}
  395: If $H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)=0$, then
  396: \[
  397: \mathrm{Hom}(H_2(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),A)=0,
  398: \]
  399: so $\mathrm{ev}_{\omega}=0$ for every $\omega$. Apply \Cref{thm:character-blind-obstructions}.
  400: \end{proof}
  401: 
  402: \begin{corollary}[No character-blind obstructions when $H_1$ is free]\label{cor:h1-free-no-blindness}
  403: Assume that $H_1(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)$ is free abelian. Then
  404: \[
  405: K_{\omega}=0
  406: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  407: \omega=0.
  408: \]
  409: Equivalently, every nonzero branch obstruction class has a nontrivial $H_2$-visible part. In particular, if $|\Vis_{p,s}(r)|=1$, every nontrivial gluing obstruction on such a nerve produces a proper finite-nerve-class visible quotient.
  410: \end{corollary}
  411: 
  412: \begin{proof}
  413: If $H_1(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z)$ is free abelian, then
  414: \[
  415: \operatorname{Ext}^1(H_1(N(\mathcal U),\mathbb Z),A)=0.
  416: \]
  417: Hence the universal coefficient map $\eta_A$ is injective. By \Cref{thm:character-blind-obstructions},
  418: \[
  419: K_{\omega}=0
  420: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  421: \mathrm{ev}_{\omega}=0
  422: \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
  423: \eta_A(\omega)=0.
  424: \]
  425: Injectivity of $\eta_A$ therefore gives $K_{\omega}=0$ if and only if $\omega=0$. If $\omega\neq 0$, then $K_{\omega}\neq 0$, so $A_{\mathrm{vis}}^{\omega}=A/K_{\omega}$ is a proper quotient of the finite group $A$.
  426: \end{proof}

  429: This is a finite-nerve mechanism example.  Let $\mathcal U$ be a finite cover
  430: whose \v{C}ech nerve is a finite triangulation of $\mathbb{R}P^2$, and take

Its only delicate step is realizing every global component section by a global stack object, and the manuscript obtains that by imposing a very strong “representative-rigid” splitting plus slice-separatedness.

id               : http://arxiv.org/abs/1701.00656v1
title            : On the Cohomology of Contextuality
updated          : 2017-01-03T11:14:35Z
link             : {link, link, link}
summary          : Recent work by Abramsky and Brandenburger used sheaf theory to give a mathematical formulation of 
                   non-locality and contextuality. By adopting this viewpoint, it has been possible to define 
                   cohomological obstructions to the existence of global sections. In the present work, we illustrate 
                   new insights into different aspects of this theory. We shed light on the power of detection of the 
                   cohomological obstruction by showing that it is not a complete invariant for strong contextuality 
                   even under symmetry and connectedness restrictions on the measurement cover, disproving a previous 
                   conjecture. We generalise obstructions to higher cohomology groups and show that they give rise to 
                   a refinement of the notion of cohomological contextuality: different "levels" of contextuality are 
                   organised in a hierarchy of logical implications. Finally, we present an alternative description of 
                   the first cohomology group in terms of torsors, resulting in a new interpretation of the 
                   cohomological obstructions.

id               : http://arxiv.org/abs/1111.3620v2
title            : The Cohomology of Non-Locality and Contextuality
updated          : 2012-10-02T00:33:41Z
link             : {link, link, link}
summary          : In a previous paper with Adam Brandenburger, we used sheaf theory to analyze the structure of 
                   non-locality and contextuality.  Moreover, on the basis of this formulation, we showed that the 
                   phenomena of non-locality and contextuality can be characterized precisely in terms of obstructions 
                   to the existence of global sections. 
                     Our aim in the present work is to build on these results, and to use the powerful tools of sheaf 
                   cohomology to study the structure of non-locality and contextuality.  We use the Cech cohomology on 
                   an abelian presheaf derived from the support of a probabilistic model, viewed as a compatible 
                   family of distributions, in order to define a cohomological obstruction for the family as a certain 
                   cohomology class.  This class vanishes if the family has a global section.  Thus the non-vanishing 
                   of the obstruction provides a sufficient (but not necessary) condition for the model to be 
                   contextual. 
                     We show that for a number of salient examples, including PR boxes, GHZ states, the Peres-Mermin 
                   magic square, and the 18-vector configuration due to Cabello et al. giving a proof of the 
                   Kochen-Specker theorem in four dimensions, the obstruction does not vanish, thus yielding 
                   cohomological witnesses for contextuality.

id               : http://arxiv.org/abs/2305.15412v1
title            : Torsors and gerbes
updated          : 2023-05-01T08:33:38Z
link             : {link, link}
summary          : Let $A$ be an abelian sheaf on a site $X_τ$ on which we have an action of a finite group $G$. Given 
                   an $A$-torsor (respectively a gerbe banded by $A$), we would like to know under what conditions it 
                   is induced from an $A^G$-torsor (respectively a gerbe banded by $A^G$). It is necessary for the 
                   torsor or the gerbe to admit a lift of the group action. Given such a $G$-action, we define an 
                   obstruction class which is a cohomology class in $H^2(G,A(X))$ for torsors and in $H^3(G,A(X))$ for 
                   gerbes. We prove that if a torsor or a gerbe is induced from the invariants, then the obstruction 
                   class is zero. Moreover, the vanishing of the obstruction class is sufficient for the reverse 
                   conclusion assuming the group cohomology classes locally vanish. The first two sheaf cohomology 
                   groups $H^i(X,A)$ can be realized concretely as the group of $A$-torsors and the group of abelian 
                   gerbes banded by $A$. Using this realization and the notion of obstruction classes, we describe 
                   morphisms of cohomologies in low degrees which come from a certain spectral sequence. A particular 
                   example of interest is the Hoschild-Serre spectral sequence that arises in the case of a finite 
                   etale Galois cover of a scheme $X$ with Galois group $G$.

title            : Bounding and simulating contextual correlations in quantum theory
updated          : 2021-06-04T16:52:58Z
link             : {link, link, link}
summary          : We introduce a hierarchy of semidefinite relaxations of the set of quantum correlations in 
                   generalised contextuality scenarios. This constitutes a simple and versatile tool for bounding the 
                   magnitude of quantum contextuality. To illustrate its utility, we use it to determine the maximal 
                   quantum violation of several noncontextuality inequalities whose maximum violations were previously 
                   unknown. We then go further and use it to prove that certain preparation-contextual correlations 
                   cannot be explained with pure states, thereby showing that mixed states are an indispensable 
                   resource for contextuality. In the second part of the paper, we turn our attention to the 
                   simulation of preparation-contextual correlations in general operational theories. We introduce the 
                   information cost of simulating preparation contextuality, which quantifies the additional, 
                   otherwise forbidden, information required to simulate contextual correlations in either classical 
                   or quantum models. In both cases, we show that the simulation cost can be efficiently bounded using 
                   a variant of our hierarchy of semidefinite relaxations, and we calculate it exactly in the simplest 
                   contextuality scenario of parity-oblivious multiplexing.

id               : http://arxiv.org/abs/2602.16716v3
title            : Contextuality from Single-State Ontological Models: An Information-Theoretic Obstruction
updated          : 2026-04-15T10:37:25Z
link             : {link, link}
summary          : Contextuality is a central feature of quantum theory, traditionally understood as the impossibility 
                   of reproducing quantum measurement statistics using noncontextual ontological models. We study 
                   classical ontological descriptions in which a fixed subsystem-level ontic state space is reused 
                   across multiple interventions. Our main result is an information-theoretic obstruction: whenever a 
                   classical single-state model reproduces operational statistics using an auxiliary contextual 
                   register, the required contextual information is lower-bounded by the conditional mutual 
                   information $I(C;O\mid λ)$ between intervention $C$ and outcome $O$ conditioned on the subsystem 
                   ontic state $λ$.
                     The mathematical inequality itself is elementary, but its interpretive significance is 
                   structural: under shared-state reuse, contextual distinctions need not be fully internalized within 
                   the subsystem ontic state alone. We provide a constructive illustration of this point and clarify 
                   how the issue should be understood as a limitation of subsystem-level classical representation, 
                   rather than as a dualism about physical reality. We further discuss how this perspective relates to 
                   ontological models and to contextuality in quantum foundations.

title            : Deep Neural Sheaf Diffusion
updated          : 2026-05-22T18:44:04Z
link             : {link, link}
summary          : Deep Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) are essential for capturing complex dependencies in 
                   graph-structured data. However, scaling GNNs to depth remains challenging, as stacking layers leads 
                   to representation collapse and diminishing sensitivity due to repeated aggregation. While Neural 
                   Sheaf Diffusion (NSD) provides strong theoretical guarantees against such collapse, these 
                   guarantees do not translate to practice: as depth increases, the disagreement signal of the sheaf 
                   Laplacian vanishes, limiting the contribution of deeper layers. We identify mechanisms that hinder 
                   NSD effectiveness at depth and propose \emph{Deep Neural Sheaf Diffusion} (DNSD), which replaces 
                   the sheaf Laplacian with a sheaf adjacency operator to maintain informative signals across layers. 
                   This is complemented by normalization, odd nonlinearities, and gating. To provide a principled 
                   explanation of the expected performance improvement, we contrast sheaf diffusion to graph attention 
                   mechanisms, highlighting that DNSD replaces scalar attention scores with matrix-valued edge 
                   functions and normalizes node representations rather than attention scores. We demonstrate 
                   empirically that DNSD effectively utilizes deep aggregation in graph tasks, outperforming GNN and 
                   NSD baselines with up to 30pp accuracy on synthetic long-range datasets, and consistently 
                   outperforming them on real-world benchmarks. These results position sheaf-based architectures as a 
                   promising building block for graph foundation models by supporting effective deep architectures.

title            : Representation of relative sheaf cohomology
updated          : 2018-10-15T06:47:53Z
link             : {link, link}
summary          : We study the cohomology theory of sheaf complexes for open embeddings of topological spaces and 
                   related subjects. The theory is situated in the intersection of the general Cech theory and the 
                   theory of derived categories. That is to say, on the one hand the cohomology is described as the 
                   relative cohomology of the sections of the sheaf complex, which appears naturally in the theory of 
                   Cech cohomology of sheaf complexes. On the other hand it is interpreted as the cohomology of a 
                   complex dual to the mapping cone of a certain morphism of complexes in the theory of derived 
                   categories. We prove a "relative de Rham type theorem" from the above two viewpoints. It says that, 
                   in the case the complex is a soft or fine resolution of a certain sheaf, the cohomology is 
                   canonically isomorphic with the relative cohomology of the sheaf. Thus the former provides a handy 
                   way of representing the latter. Along the way we develop various theories and establishes canonical 
                   isomorphisms among the cohomologies that appear therein. The second viewpoint leads to a 
                   generalization of the theory to the case of cohomology of sheaf morphisms. Some special cases 
                   together with applications are also indicated.

title            : Lattice Gerbe Theory
updated          : 2014-09-30T19:50:34Z
link             : {link, link, link}
summary          : We formulate the theory of a 2-form gauge field on a Euclidean spacetime lattice. In this approach, 
                   the fundamental degrees of freedom live on the faces of the lattice, and the action can be 
                   constructed from the sum over Wilson surfaces associated with each fundamental cube of the lattice. 
                   If we take the gauge group to be $U(1)$, the theory reduces to the well-known abelian gerbe theory

                   gauge group $U(N) \times U(N)$, which gives rise to $U(N)$ Yang-Mills theory upon dimensional 
                   reduction. Formulating the theory on a lattice has several other advantages. In particular, it is 
                   possible to compute many observables, such as the expectation value of Wilson surfaces, 
                   analytically at strong coupling and numerically for any value of the coupling.

codex
The literature search confirms that the surrounding program is established: Abramsky–Mansfield–Barbosa already define Čech cohomology obstructions to global sections, Carù studies their detection limits and torsor interpretation, and later work develops complete/semi-module and bundle formulations. I’m checking whether the manuscript’s gerbe and quotient constructions add an invariant with independent content, or only apply standard \(H^2\), UCT, annihilator, and subgroup formulas to newly named semantic data.

id               : http://arxiv.org/abs/2104.11411v2
title            : Characterization of Contextuality with Semi-Module Čech Cohomology and its Relation with Cohomology 
                   of Effect Algebras
updated          : 2021-09-04T15:03:25Z
link             : {link, link}
summary          : I present a generalized notion of obstruction in Čech cohomology on semi-modules, which allows one 
                   to characterize non-disturbing contextual behaviors with any semi-field. This framework generalizes 
                   the usual Čech cohomology used in the sheaf approach to contextuality, avoiding false positives. I 
                   revise a similar work done in the framework of effect algebras with cyclic and order cohomologies 
                   and explore the relationship with the one presented here.

id               : http://arxiv.org/abs/1602.04552v1
title            : Extending the Nested Parallel Model to the Nested Dataflow Model with Provably Efficient Schedulers
updated          : 2016-02-15T02:46:59Z
link             : {link, link}
summary          : The nested parallel (a.k.a. fork-join) model is widely used for writing parallel programs. However, 
                   the two composition constructs, i.e. "$\parallel$" (parallel) and "$;$" (serial), are insufficient 
                   in expressing "partial dependencies" or "partial parallelism" in a program. We propose a new 
                   dataflow composition construct "$\leadsto$" to express partial dependencies in algorithms in a 
                   processor- and cache-oblivious way, thus extending the Nested Parallel (NP) model to the 
                   \emph{Nested Dataflow} (ND) model. We redesign several divide-and-conquer algorithms ranging from 
                   dense linear algebra to dynamic-programming in the ND model and prove that they all have optimal 
                   span while retaining optimal cache complexity. We propose the design of runtime schedulers that map 
                   ND programs to multicore processors with multiple levels of possibly shared caches (i.e, Parallel 
                   Memory Hierarchies) and provide theoretical guarantees on their ability to preserve locality and 
                   load balance. For this, we adapt space-bounded (SB) schedulers for the ND model. We show that our 
                   algorithms have increased "parallelizability" in the ND model, and that SB schedulers can use the 
                   extra parallelizability to achieve asymptotically optimal bounds on cache misses and running time 
                   on a greater number of processors than in the NP model. The running time for the algorithms in this 
                   paper is $O\left(\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{h-1} Q^{*}({\mathsf t};σ\cdot M_i)\cdot C_i}{p}\right)$, where 
                   $Q^{*}$ is the cache complexity of task ${\mathsf t}$, $C_i$ is the cost of cache miss at level-$i$ 
                   cache which is of size $M_i$, $σ\in(0,1)$ is a constant, and $p$ is the number of processors in an 
                   $h$-level cache hierarchy.
category         : category
published        : 2016-02-15T02:46:59Z
primary_category : primary_category
author           : {David Dinh, Harsha Vardhan Simhadri, Yuan Tang}

id               : http://arxiv.org/abs/2402.01542v4
title            : Learning Collective Variables with Synthetic Data Augmentation through Physics-Inspired Geodesic 
                   Interpolation
updated          : 2024-07-19T17:48:10Z
link             : {link, link, link}
summary          : In molecular dynamics simulations, rare events, such as protein folding, are typically studied 
                   using enhanced sampling techniques, most of which are based on the definition of a collective 
                   variable (CV) along which acceleration occurs. Obtaining an expressive CV is crucial, but often 
                   hindered by the lack of information about the particular event, e.g., the transition from unfolded 
                   to folded conformation. We propose a simulation-free data augmentation strategy using 
                   physics-inspired metrics to generate geodesic interpolations resembling protein folding 
                   transitions, thereby improving sampling efficiency without true transition state samples. This new 
                   data can be used to improve the accuracy of classifier-based methods. Alternatively, a 
                   regression-based learning scheme for CV models can be adopted by leveraging the interpolation 
                   progress parameter.
category         : {category, category, category}
published        : 2024-02-02T16:35:02Z
primary_category : primary_category
author           : {Soojung Yang, Juno Nam, Johannes C. B. Dietschreit, Rafael Gómez-Bombarelli}
doi              : 10.1021/acs.jctc.4c00435

id               : http://arxiv.org/abs/2207.06065v1
title            : Machine-learning effective many-body potentials for anisotropic particles using 
                   orientation-dependent symmetry functions
updated          : 2022-07-13T09:19:43Z
link             : {link, link, link}
summary          : Spherically-symmetric atom-centered descriptors of atomic environments have been widely used for 
                   constructing potential or free energy surfaces of atomistic and colloidal systems and to 
                   characterize local structures using machine learning techniques. However, when particle shapes are 
                   non-spherical, as in the case of rods and ellipsoids, standard spherically-symmetric structure 
                   functions alone produce imprecise descriptions of local environments. In order to account for the 
                   effects of orientation, we introduce two- and three-body orientation-dependent particle-centered 
                   descriptors for systems composed of rod-like particles. To demonstrate the suitability of the 
                   proposed functions, we use an efficient feature selection scheme and simple linear regression to 
                   construct coarse-grained many-body interaction potentials for computationally-efficient simulations 
                   of model systems consisting of colloidal particles with anisotropic shape: mixtures of colloidal 
                   rods and nonadsorbing polymer, hard rods enclosed by an elastic microgel shell, and 
                   ligand-stabilized nanorods. We validate the machine-learning (ML) effective many-body potentials 
                   based on orientation-dependent symmetry functions by using them in direct coexistence simulations 
                   to map out the phase behavior of colloidal rods and non-adsorbing polymer. We find good agreement 
                   with results obtained from simulations of the true binary mixture, demonstrating that the effective 
                   interactions are well-described by the orientation-dependent ML potentials.
category         : category
published        : 2022-07-13T09:19:43Z
primary_category : primary_category
author           : {Gerardo Campos-Villalobos, Giuliana Giunta, Susana Marín-Aguilar, Marjolein Dijkstra}
doi              : 10.1063/5.0091319

id               : http://arxiv.org/abs/1807.04203v1
title            : Towards a complete cohomology invariant for non-locality and contextuality
updated          : 2018-07-11T15:40:57Z
link             : {link, link}
summary          : The sheaf theoretic description of non-locality and contextuality by Abramsky and Brandenburger 
                   sets the ground for a topological study of these peculiar features of quantum mechanics. This 
                   viewpoint has been recently developed thanks to sheaf cohomology, which provides a sufficient 
                   condition for contextuality of empirical models in quantum mechanics and beyond. Subsequently, a 
                   number of studies proposed methods to detect contextuality based on different cohomology theories. 
                   However, none of these cohomological descriptions succeeds in giving a full invariant for 
                   contextuality applicable to concrete examples. In the present work, we introduce a cohomology 
                   invariant for possibilistic and strong contextuality which is applicable to the vast majority of 
                   empirical models.
category         : category
published        : 2018-07-11T15:40:57Z
comment          : 46 pages, 25 figures

The main referee vulnerability is sharper than “limited novelty”: the realization/classification chain relies on a bespoke formal Čech site being asserted to have the desired derived cohomology and genuine constant-band behavior. If that comparison fails or is merely stipulated through the admissible-expansion convention, the headline becomes an encoding exercise rather than a theorem about the original state-forcing semantics.
